Subject:
Korean Reaction to Rumored Extension of Japanese
Fishing Limits.
FR
DYR
The Honorable
FE
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
OLI
4/100
Sir:
I have the honor to report on Korean official and public
Pos
reaction to rumors of possible extension of the so-called
174
"MacArthur Line" delimiting Japanese fishing.
ECa
Korean Kavy Chief of Staff SON Won Il made the first recent
reference to the "MacArthur Line" in e newspaper statement of
June 2nd: ". . . It occurs to me that the MacArthur Line might,
in fact, be removed in consideration of the recent notification
of GHQ ScaP stating that Korean Navy authorities should not
capture any Japanese ships, but only report the name of them to
GHa, so that GHQ itself may punish them . . ." Admiral Son con-
tinued (referring to Japanese violations of the SCAP fishing limit),
. . . we are pressingly in need of more guard-ships adequate for
tightening the coast-guard against the Japanese violation of our
coastline."
On June Sth, the Chosun Ilbo (politically middle-of-the-road)
commented on "secret illegal fishing" by the Japanese beyond the
"MacArthur Line" along the Korean coast. The newspaper article
continued by reporting the Japanese petition, and a report that the
Philippine Government had protested to SCAP. The article concluded
with a reported statement of Foreign Minister Ben LIMB: " . . . We
cannot tolerate Japan's attempt to extend the /MacArthur line.
Receiving such an outrageous report, the /Foreign Affairs Ministry
is negotiating with SCAP through our mission in Japan in order to
prevent the extension of the line."
The National Independence Federation's propaganda chief was
quoted in the Sisa Sinmoon (a government-supporting paper) of
June 9th to the effect that "such an intrigue as the maneuvering
for an expansion of the MacArthur Line is nothing but a proof that
Japanese imperialistic ambitions are not yet extirpated. . . The
entire population of Korea should unite in order to stop the ful-
fillment of such a plot by the Japanese." (The National Independence
Federation is Dr. KIMM Kiu Sic's middle-of-the-road group.

American Embassy,
June 20, 1949

Seoul, Despatch No. 366
Tong-A Ilbo (a large, extreme-right paper) of June llth reported
the dispatch of a telegram by Foreign Minister Limb to CHuNG Han Pum,
Chief of the Korean Diplomatic Mission in Japan, directing CHUNG to
consult with SCAF. At a press conference on June 14th, Minister Limb
1
announced the contents of the telegram. Six points were to be stressed
in the consultations: (1) Japanese invasion of the MacArthur Line
threatened the Korean fishing industry; (2) extension of the Line
would "expedite the second rising of Japan"; (3) extension of the Line
would threaten Korean national defense; (4) since Japan "has unlimited
fishing area on her eastern coast," she had an ulterior motive in
seeking extension; (5) revision of the Line without Korean consent
would "weaken the defense of the Far Fest; (6) fishing areas "now
owned by Korea ere an important part of Korean economy."
President Riee, asked at his press conference on June loth for
his thoughts on the Japanese petition, said: "/The Japanese probably
would like to extend themselves even to Seoul. They had a good time
for forty years in Korea. But the Korean people will oppose them."
Editorial comment, as represented by the Kyunghang Sinmoon
23
(extreme-right Catholic paper) of June 9th and the Chosun IIbo (middle-
of-the-road) of June l4th, reflected Korean hypersensitivity to all
developments in Japen and opposition to anything which could con-
ceivably strengthen the Japanese nation. In addition, the editorials
used the "MacArthur Line" to illustrate the thesis that the United
States is being too good to Japan. Kyunghang said, ". . . Japanese
imperialism is now growing under the protection of the United States.
. . . Should the United States approve the petition of the Japanese
overnment, she would lose the confidence of the Asian peoples as a
whole."
Officers of Diplomatic Section, SCAP, in telephone conversation
4
with this Embassy on June 16th stated that SCAP had no intention of
extending the "MacArthur Line" until the Japanese had proved their
ability to obey regulations within present limits. Moreover, even if
the line were to be extended, the Diplomatic Section stated, the ex-
tension would be to the south and east, toward the trusteeship area,
and not toward Korea. They reported further that Chief of Mission
Chung had called upon the Acting Political Advisor on June llth, and
had been informed that there was no truth in the rumor that the "Mac-
Arthur Line" would be extended toward Korea.
On June 16th Mr. HONG Sung Chin, Chief of the Second Section,
5
Policy Bureau, Ministry of Foreign Affairs, called at the Embassy
and asked whether the MacArthur Line would be extended. Although
he was assured repeatedly that it would not, he seemed unconvinced.
6
The following day his subordinate came again with a note requesting
"written information" from the Embassy. The note was answered on
June 20th with the statement that all information on the subject had
been given to the Chief of the Korean diplomatic mission in Japan.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Everett F. Drumright

UNITED STATES INFORMATION SERVICE
AMERICAN EMBASSY
Seoul, Korea
PRESS INTERVIEW
Mr. Ben Limb, Minister of Forein Affairs, met pressmen at 10s4o
on 14 June 1949 in a twenty-minute interview when the following
questions and answers were given:
. The United States Government showed a favorable attitude
toward the Japanese extension of the MacArthur Line. What is your
comment?
A. This problem brcut ort comments as early as last May.
I learned that Japanese Premier Voshida had conferred with General
HacArthur and requested an extensien of the fishing area. We felt
that this directly concerned Korea so sent instructions to our
Ambassador, r. Chung an u, lat in ay. Contents of the tel
gram are as follows:
Japanese covernmont made several petitions regarding the
extension of acArthur Line. I instruct you to negotiate with
SCAP stressing the following pcints:
1. Japanese fishers' invasion of NacArthur Line means
deprivation oi our rights. There is no reason to fear that
Japanese fichers may threaten the fishing industry of Korea.
2. Extension of acArthur Line will expedite the second
rising of Japan, and threaten the peace of the Far East.
3. Extension of the MacArthur Line threatens the national
defense of Korea.
L. Japan has unlinited fishing area on her eastern coast
and we think Japan's intention to extend the Line has objec-
tives other than greater fishing area.
5. Revision of the MacArthur Line without consent of
Korea will weaken the defense of the Far East.
6. Fishing areas now owned by Korea are an important part
of Korean economy.
Q. Foreign news despatches indicate that U.S. troops will with-
draw from Kcrea soon. Do you know how many members of the military
mission will remain here?
A. Withdrgwal of J.S. troops is in progress and more than one
hundred persons of the military mission will remain here, I guess.
I want to tell you, an independent country does not need foreign
troops, but the United States must fulfill its responsibilities
before the troops leave.
Q. The British

Q. The British and French Governments revealed that they
intend to recognize the Chinese Communist Government. What is
your policy?
A. The Republic of Korea cannot recognize the Chinese Red
Government.
Q. Recently many Chinese displaced persons arriyed in Korea
due to their Civil War. What is your counter-measure?
A. The Department of Commerce and other departments concerned
are investigating all displaced persons and permit proper persons
entry into Korea. Some Chinese who supported our Provisional
Government in Shanghai during the Japanese domination have arrived
here.

Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 366, dated June 20, 1949, from
American Embassy, Seoul, Korea.
THE WHOLE NATION PROTESTS AGAINST THE EXTENSION OF MACARTHUR LINE.
(EDITORIAL)
(June 9) (KYUNGHYANG) Of late, the Japanese government
formally handed to MacArthur's Headquarters a petition calling
for the enlargement of the MacArthur line so that the Japanese
may enjoy more extensive fishing grounds in the Yellow Sea than
now. Originally, the MacArthur line was devised by the Allied
Powers for the purpose of restricting the activities of the
Japanese fishing ships to the home waters of Japan. We cannot but
see the petition asking for the right of the Japanese fishing ships
to enter Korean waters as a trick of the Japanese Government to re-
invade the continent.
Although a defeated country, Japanese imperialism is now
growing under the protection of the United States. MacArthur's
Headquarters issued an instruction, "Respect the Japanese", and
is endeavouring to raise the standing of the Japanese rapidly.
This is still vivid in our memory. Taking advantage of the change
of the US policy toward Japan from the spirit of the Potsdam
Declaration to a policy of upbringing, the Japanese Government
petitioned the expansion of the MacArthur Line under the cloak
of the alleviation of the food situation.
If such an arrogant demand of the Japanese governnent is
granted, this will implant a seed of calamity in Asia.
Viewed purely from an economic standpoint, we cannot but call
the petition "outrageous". Since Kerea has a predominant right
to fishery in both the Yellow and East Seas, they are indispensable
to our country. Fishery ranks second to agriculture in Korean
economic importance. Therefore, allowing the Japanese fishing
ships to come into the Yellow and East Seas would mean the crippling
of the Korean economy. For this reason, we oppose the expansion of
the MacArthur line.
SCAP may take up the petition from a political standpoint, but
we assert that the anti-Japanese sentiment of the Asian people has
not yet subsided. Should the US approve the petition of the
Japanese government, she would lose the confidence of the Asian
peoples as a whole.
From this broad point of view, we earnestly request of SCAP
to refuse the expansion of the MacArthur line. As the past history
teaches us, it was when Japan gained the command of the sea that
the Japanese imperialism started its invasion activities in earnest.
Now, the whole nation has made full preparations for the protest
against the expansion of the MacArthur line from political and
economical standpoints. What Japan really wants is not marine pro-
ducts, but the expansion of her influence. We cannot acquiesce in
the trick of the Japanese government to encroach on the Yellow and
East Seas from the standpoint of self-defense. Therefore, we hope
that SCAP will take an adequate step against the petition.
UNCLASSIFIED

Enclosure No. 3 to Despatch No. 366, dated June 20, 1949, from
American Embassy, Seoul.
THE CHANGE OF THE US POLICY TOWARD JAPAN
(EDITORIAL)
(CHOSUN) Simultaneously with the end of the last war and
immediately after the occupation by the US forces of Japan, Gen.
MacArthur, as is universally known, was fully authorized to
implement the Allied policy toward Japan as the Commander-in-chief
of the Allied forces. Also, there is no doubt that the MacArthur
policy has been gradually changing.from its original course as
time passes--that is to say that the MacArthur's policy, formerly
based on the joint will of the Allied Powers, appears to be based
on the unilateral will of the United States.
Since the United States apparently played the greatest role
in the fighting against Japan, it must be admitted as a matter
of course that she should take a lead in the Allied policy toward
Japan. But this, I would dare say, does not put anyone under an
obligation to keep mum about the US policy toward Japan. Especial
for Korea, a neighbor of Japan, it is a metter of great concern.
Taking major examples from a series of the latest events that
occurred in relation to Japan, the United States offered the
abandonment of Japanese reparations, while Japan asked for the
expansion of the MacArthur line. Of course, we know well why the
United States intends to lighten her burdens in relation to Japan.
Also, we admit that there are points which the United States should
take into consideration for the benefit of Japan's existence.
However, we cannot agree unconditionally to United States intentions
of making Japan a strategic base. In particular, the expansion of
the MacArthur line is connected with the interests of Korea. We,
therefore, cannot but have a word on the US policy toward Japan,
so long as the United States follows a policy of "Japan first".
Not only the United States but also all Far Eastern countries have
a full understanding as to what kind of role Japanese technique
will play in the Orient. However, Japanese technique must be
restricted to a certain extent; sacrificing the neighboring countries
of Japan for the benefit of Japan's existence would mean endanger-
ing Oriental peace as well as world peace. I, therefore, hope that
the US government and General MacArthur will take into consideration
the above-mentioned points.
UNCLASSIFIED

Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 366, dated June 20, 1949, from
American Embassy, Seoul.
MEMORANDUM OF TELEPHONE CONVERSATION
June 15, 1949
Time: 10:30 a.m., June 15, 1949.
Participants: D. S. Macdonald, Seoul - Mr. Richard B. FINN, Tokyo,
Diplomatic Section, GHQ. SCAP
Subject: Extension of "MacArthur Line"
Mr. Finn made the following statements:
Ambassador CHUNG of Korea called on Mr. Huston Saturday.
June 1lth, and was informed that there was no truth in the rumor
that the fishing line would be extended toward Korea.
Following are facts pertaining to the situation:
SCAP received authority from Washington in January to extend
the fishing line south and east toward the American trustee area;
this would be toward the Philippines. (The Chief of the Philippine
Mission in Japan also called on Mr. Huston, and is most concerned
over the extension of the line toward the Philippines.) They
absolutely do not intend to extend the line toward Korea or China.
The authority for extension hes been kept fairly confidential
because SCAP does not intend to extend the line until the Japanese
have proved that they can comply with present restrictions, which
they are deinitely not complying with at present.
Apparently news of the authority has leaked out, as is indicated
by the sudden flood of petitions and publicity being received now
for extension of the line; evidently the very large and powerful
fishing industry is mainly responsible for the publicity, but is
acting througi both the Diet and the Executive Branch.
SCAP also desires to keep all punitive authority for violation
of fishing restrictions within the jurisdiction of SCAP or the
Japanese in order, partially, to protect the Japanese fishing people
and prevent international complications --- the Russians and Chinese
have already attacked Japanese fishing vessels who had wandered
beyond their legal fishing boundaries, and Koreans are not entirely
innocent of such a charge.
Tokyo will henceforth forward copies of any correspondence on
extension of the MacArthur Line to the Embassy, Seoul.
NOTE: On June 16th, in a second telephone call, Mr. Finn stated to
Mr. Macdonald that the petition which had apparently aroused the
present flood of comment was a Japanese Diet resolution of late May
or early June; but that SCAP had received hundreds of petitions from
various groups, public, commercial, and private, over a period of
several months.

Enclosure No. 5 to Despatch No. 366, dated June 20, 1949, from
American Embassy, Seoul.
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
June 17, 1949
Subject: "MacArthur Line" del imiting Japanese fishing
Participants: Mr. HONG Sung Chin, Chief, Second Section,
Policy Bureau, Ministry of Foreign Affairs.
Mr. KIM Ch'i Soon, Assistant to Mr. Hong.
D. S. Macdonald, Vice-Consul, Political Section.
Place and Time: Room 505, mbassy Building, 2:30 p.m., June 16, 1949.
Mr. Hong and Mr. Kim called at Mr. Macdonald's office by appoint-
ment made through Mr. HUTCHINSON in regard to the MacArthur Line.
Mr. Hong, after very brief preliminaries, inquired whether the
MacArthur Line would be extended by SCAP, or whether it had been ex-
tended. He referred to recent "foreign news reports" concerning the
extension of the Line, and seemed to labor under the impression that
SCAP planned to extend it.
Mr. Macdonald stated that he had communicated by telephone with
the Diplomatic Section, SCAP, end had received definite assurances
that SCAP did not plan to extend the MacArthur Line at this time.
He added that the news reports were apparently based on a petition
in the fom of a resolution of the Jepenese Diet.This petition, how-
ever, was only one of lundreds of petitions sent to SCAP over a period
of several months. Mr. Macdonald continued that even if, at some
future time, SCAP should reconsider the problem of extending Japanese
fishing limits, the extension would be toward the east and south,
and not toward Korea. He further stated that this information had
been communicated to the Chief of the Korean Diplomatic Mission in
Tokyo, Mr. CHUNG, on the occasion of Mr. Chung's call at the Diplo-
matic Section, SCAF, on June 1l.
Mr. Hong asked several times over whether the MacArthur Line
would be extended, and although he appeared to understand the
negative answer given him, he seemed somewhet unconvinced of the
truth of Mr. Macdonald's statements.
NOTE: On June 17th, Mr. KIM Ch'i Soon came again to Mr. Macdonald's
office, bearing a memorandum from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs
requestin written assurances from the Enbassy, which Mr. Kim stated
would be released to the press by the Ministry.

Enclosure No. 7 to Despatch No. 366, dated June 20, 1949, from
American Embassy, Seoul.
No. 15
The American Enbassy presents its compliments to the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs, and has the honor to make reference to the
Ministry's note number FB-2-00136, June 18, 1949, concerning
alteration of the "MacArthur Line" delimiting Japanese fishing.
According to telephone conversation with members of the staff
of the Acting Political Advisor to the Supreme Commander, Allied
Powers, in Tokyo, the Acting Political Advisor discussed the prob-
lem of the "Macarthur Line" at some length with the Chief of the
Korean Diplomatic Mission in Japan and communicated to him the
position of the Supreme Commander, Allied Powers, with respect to
the limitation of Japanese fishing.
It is therefore suggested that the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs utilize the report of the Chief of the Korean Diplomatic
Mission in Japan as a basis for further infonmation on this subject.
The American Embassy,
Seoul, June 20, 1949.

Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Department's instruotfon
no. 16 of June 1, 1949, concerning the ultimate
disposition of Formosa and the Kuril Islands and
possible United States support to Japanese claims at
the peace settlement to the Habomai Group, Shikotan,
Kunashiri, and Etorofu islands, I have the honor to
transmit a memorandum, dated June 15, 1949, prepared
by FSO Charles N. SPINKS on the subject of Soviet and
Japanese territorial claims to the Kuril Islands, the
Habomai Islands, and Shikotan Island. It is believed
that this memorandum affords a useful resumé of historioal
claims to these territories end contemporary developments
under the Occupation which may have a close bearing on
their ultimate disposition.
The problem of the future disposition of the Kuril-
Habomai-Shikotan area has been brought to the immediato
attention of this Mission through assertions of terri-
torial sovereignty over these islands and adjacent waters
made by the Soviet Member, Allied Counoil for Japan, in
connection with the seizure and detention of Japanese
fishing vessels allegedly operating outside the authorized
fishing area established by General Headquarters (this
XK
Mission's despatches nos. 706 of November 2, 1948, and
np
34 of January 19, 1949).
The three-power agreement signed at Yalta on
C
FILED
February 11, 1945, 1s the primary basis of the Soviet
91949
Union's present position in southern Sakhalin and the
Kuril Islands. This agreement provided for the entry of
the Soviet Union into war against Japan two or three
months
2
SECRET
RETURN TO DO/R PILES WITHN 14 DAYS, WITN A MOTATION OF AOTION TAE.

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
months after termination of the war in Europe, on several
conditions one of which was that "The Kuril islands shall
be handed over to the Soviet Union." There was, however,
no definition of the term Kuril Islands. Since the
Habomai Islands and Shikotan Island,have been traditionally
regarded as an island group distinct from the Kuril
archipelago, and under Japanese control had a lodal administra-
tion as a political subdivision of Hokkaido separate from
the Kuril island local administration, there appears no
valid basis, either in the Yalta Agreement or in any
other international understanding, justifying Soviet
occupation of the Habomai-Shikotan a rea at the end of the
war in addition to the Soviet occupation of the Kuril
Islands. It can only be concluded, therefore, that the
Soviet occupation of the Habomai Group and Shikotan
Island was a unilateral action for which no more previous
understanding had been reached than for Soviet occupation
of the island of Hokkaido.
Although the Yalta Agreement did not specifically
provide for the actual occupation by Soviet forces of
the Kuril Islands (or any other area), it did of course
contemplate military operations against Japanese foroes
after the Soviet Union's entry into the war against
Japan (which took place on August 8, 1945). The Yalta
Agreement's provision for the ultimate disposition of
the Kuril Islands unquestionably afforded ample justifi-
cation for the invasion and occupation of this area by
Soviet forces. This action was, of course, recognized
in General Order No. 1 issued to the Japanese Government
by the Supreme Commander on September 2, 1945, which
provided that Japanese forces in Manchuria, North Korea,
southem Sakhalin, and the Kuril Islands were to surrender
to the Commander-in-Chief of Soviet Forces in the Far East.
From Japanese sources it has been learned, however,
that on the basis of a surrender agreement concluded
on August 19, 1945, in Manchuria between the Commander-in-
Chief of Soviet Forces in the Far East and the Chief of
Staff of the Japanese Kwantung Army, Japanese forces through-
out the entire Kuril chain and in the Habomai Group and
Shikotan had ceased hostilities and surrendered their
arms before General Order No. 1 was issued on September 2.
It may be said, therefore, that the Soviet Union, while
invading and occupying the Kuril Islands, lost no time
in extending this military operation to include the
Habomai-Shikotan area, thus confronting the Allied Powers,
and particularly the United States, with a fait acoompli.
The Basic Initial Post-Surrender Directive to SCAP
for the Occupation and Control of Japan, prepared by the
Joint Chiefs of Staff (JCS Directive No. 18 of November 8,
1945, Section 4, paragraph d) provides for the complete
governmental and administrative separation from Japan of
certain Pacific islands acquired by Japan since l914,
Manchuria,
SECRET

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
Le patch no. 41 6,
Jame 27, 19 9.
SECRET
-3-
Manchuria, Formosa, the Pescadores, Korea, Karafuto
(southern Sakhalin), and "such other territories as may
be specified in future directives." This provision of
JCS 18, which it may be noted did not specifically mention
the Kuril Islands, was carried out by General Headquarters
through a directive to the Japanese Government on
January 29, 1946 (SCAFIN 677). In defining the areas to
be separated from Japan governmentally and administratively,
SCAPIN 677 specifically enumerates the Kuril Islands as
well as the Habomai Group and Shikotan Island. Historically,
this is the first time that the Habomai-Shikotan area is
dealt with in a document of international character con-
cerned with the territorial boundaries of Japan. Although
the Habomai-Shikotan area was under Soviet occupation
at the time SCAPIN 677 was drafted, such specific mention
of the area in this directive has unfortunately tended to
endorse if not validate the Soviet position there. In
a sense, therefo e, it is believed that SCAPIN 677, while
fundamentally a General Headquarters administrative matter,
will be regarded by the Soviet Government as tantamount
to an addendum to or extension of the provisions of the
Yalta Agreement.
The establishment by the Supreme Commander of the
authorized Japanese fishing area through SCAPIN 1033
of June 22, 1946, as amended, wil1 also no doubt be
cited as supporting the Soviet position in the Kuril Islands
and the Habomai-Shikotan area by its exclusion of these
islands from the scope of the fishing area.
From the historical standpoint, Japan is believed
to have a sound claim to the Kuril and Habomai-Shikotan
islands which is, of course, completely disregarded by
the Yalta Agreement. Historical Japanese records and
extant old maps plainly indicate that from at least the
beginning of the 17th century the Japanese regarded the
Kuril Islands and the Habomai-Shikotan area as Japanese
territory. Moreover, there is abundant historioal evidenoe
to show that the Japanese through almost the entire
Tokugawa period (16o0-l86e) provided some degree of
political administration for the Habomai Group, Shikotan,
and the islands of Kunashiri and Etorofu in the Kuril
archipelago. In contrast, the earliest known Russian
activity in the Kuril Islands did not begin until early
in the l8th century, and there is little evidence of real
Russian political administration in any of-tle islands
until the 19th century。
The first treaty between Japan and Russia, signed
at Shimoda in 1855, clearly divided the Kuril Islands
between the two countries at the Etorofu Straits. By
the Saint Petersburg Treaty of l875, Japan, in exchange
for relinquishing her rights and interests in Sakhalin
to Russia, received from Russia the Kuril Islands
northeast
SECRET

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
northeast of the Etorofu Straits. Thereafter, the Kuril
Islunds were never the subject of dispute or discussion
between the two countries. The Habomai Islands and
Shikotan Island, on the other hand, were never discussed
in connection with the treaty settiements in 1855 and 1875,
their Japanese status never theretofore having been
disputed
With the establishment of Japan's modern system
of political subdivisions and administration after the
Meiji Restoration in 1868, the Kuril Islands in the
possession of Japan were made integral parts of the
Hokkaido prefectural government, forming various dis-
tricts of Chishima-gun, or Chishima County, which is in
turn part of the Nemuro Shicho, or Nemuro Branch Administra-
tion The Habomai Islands and Shikotan Island constituted
another administrative subdivision of the Nemuro Shicho.
There appears to be a tendency to think of the so-
called "minor outlying islands" off the Japanese coasts
independently of their historical relationships to Japan
and the long periods over which some of them have formed
integral parts of the Japanese home administration. It
is believed that any peace settlement with Japan should
give adequate weight to these important historical and
administrative considerations.
In the case of the Kuril Islands, the Habomai
Group, and Shikotan Island, their permanent transfer to
the Soviet Union would necessitate complete disregard
of Japanese territorial claims which are virtually as
valid as the Japanese claim to the island of Hokkaido.
It is therefore believed that, in the event of a peace
settlement, Japan is reasonably entitled to expect some
readjustment of the territorial provisions of the Yalta
Agreement, as now supplemented by the provisions and
implications of SCAPIN 677 and SCAPIN 1O33. It is
believed that consideration should be given to the fact
that, of the Kuril Islands, Etorofu and Kunashiri have
always been regarded as Japanese territory, a faot which
Russia formally recognized in its earliest treaty with
Japan, the Shimoda Treaty of 1855; and that the Habomai
Islands and Shikotan Island have likewise always been
regarded as Japanese territory, their possession by Japan
never having been previously contested or violated until
the unilateral occupation of the area by Soviet forces
in 1945。
There are also believed to be other important
considerations which would justify territorial readjust-
ments in this area. The present administrative boundary
between Japan and Soviet-occupied islands off the eastern
coast
SECRET

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
Sot
-5-
coast of Hokkaido is a tortuous line running through
narrow, fog-bound channels which during the fishing
season bear considerable traffic. If this line becomes
a permanent boundary there will unquestionably occur
continuous violations of Soviet waters by Japanese
craft and violations of Japanese waters by Soviet shipping.
The separation from Japan of the southern Kuril Islands,
the Habomai Group, and Shikotan also deprives the
Hokkaido fisheries of an important fishing area, the loss
of which represents an undesirable restriction on Japan's
overtaxed facilities for producing foodstuffs. Finally,
it should also be recognized that Soviet possession of
Kunashiri Island and the Habomai Group brings Soviet
power within exceedingly close proximity of metropolitan
Japan, a situation which the Japanese cannot help but
regard as a grave strategic liability.
It is believed that the most satisfactory and effective
territorial adjustment could be made by restoring to
Japan the two southernmost Kuril Islands of Etorofu and
Kunashiri, the Habomai Islands, and Shikotan Island.
The relinquishment of this territory, totalling 1,868.69
square miles, could be no substantial loss or burden to
the Soviet Union, while its future possession by Japan
could not conceivably represent a strategic threat to
the Soviet Union or materially assist Japan's hypothetical
revival as a military power. On the other hand, the
recovery of this territory by Japan is in conformity
with valid historical consideration, would enable Japan
to rehabilitate the vital Hokkaido fisheries, and would
eliminate an impractical and controversial political
boundary between Japanese and Soviet territory.
Respectfully yours,
Cloyce K. Huston
Charge d'Affaires ad interim

MEMORANDUM
June 15, 1949
Subject:
Soviet and Japanese Territorial Claims to
the Kuril Islands, the Hubomai Islands,
and Shikotan Island.
The Kuril Islands (known as the Chishima archipelago
in Japanese) are a chain of 13 major islands along with
a number of smaller islets and rocks extending from
Shimushu Island off the southern tip of the Kamchatka
Peninusla to Kunashiri Island off the eastern coast of
Hokkaido. This island chain totals 3,800.06 square
miles, the largest of the islands being Etorofu,
1,243.24 square miles. In 1940 the Kuril Islands had
a population of about 1l,400 permanent Japanese residents,
supplemented by an additional 20, O00 fishermen from
Hokkaido and northern Honshu during the fishing season.
The Habonai Islands (also known as the Suisho or
Goyomai islands) are a small group of five islands
lying off the Nosappu Peninsula in southeastern Hokkaido
The small chain totals only 19.28 square miles over
half of which (1l.96 square miles) is represented by
Shibotsu Island Further to the east is the larger island
of Shikotan (19.3 square miles) which is sometimes regarded
as a part of the Hebomai Group Before the war the Habomai-
Shikotan area had a permanent population of approximately
4,400 Japanese settlers, augmented during the fishing
season by fishermen from Hokkaido.
1。
Soviet Charges of Violations of Soviet
Territorial Waters in the Kuril, Habomai,
and Shikotan Areas.
Movements of Japanese fishing vessels outside the
limits of the authorized fishing area established by
direction of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
on June 22, 1946 (SCAPIN 1033, as amended by SCAPIN 1033/1
of December 23, 1948) have provided the Soviet Member,
Allied Council for Japan, an opportunity to advance the
claim that the Soviet Union at present possesses
territorial
SECRET

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.99)
'osure t Tokyo's
Desratch no
SECRET
-2-
territorial sovereignty in southern Sakhalin, the Kuril
Islands, the Habomai Group, Shikotan Island, and in
waters adjoining these islands which the Soviet Union
occupied at t he end of the war
Between May and October, 1948, Soviet coastal
patrols in the Sakhalin-Kurii area took into custody à
number of Japanese fishing vessels While considerable.
discrepancies exist as to the reported positions of these
vessels at times of seizure, on October 19, 1948,
the Soviet Member, Allied Council for Japan, addressed
a letter to the Supreme Commander in which protest was
made against the alleged violations of "Soviet territorial
waters" by Japanese fishing vessels. As.de from raising
the important question of Soviet territorial sovereignty
in these areas and adjacent waters, the Soviet Member's
letter of October 19 also implied that local Soviet
authorities have the right to take punitive action against
Japanese vessels which violate the limits of the authorized
fishing area
On November 2, 1948, this Mission in its capacity
as the Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters sent
a reply to the Soviet Member The question of Soviet
territorial sovereignty in the areas.and waters concerned
was ignored, but the Soviet Member was informed that it
is the responsibility of the Supreme Commander rather
than that of any individual Power to enforce directives
to the Japanese Government issued by the Supreme Commander,
and that any movements of Japanese vessels outside the area
established by SCAPIN l033 should be dealt with by the
Supreme Commander as the sole responsible authority. A
further conmunication from the Soviet Member, dated
November 27, 1948, rejected this contention as unsatisfactory
since the issue was not, from the Soviet viewpoint, con-
cerned with Japanese violations of the fishing area, but
Japanese violations of "Soviet territorial waters".
The Soviet Member has, therefore, rested the Soviet
position entirely upon the assumption that the Soviet
Union·exercises territorial sovereignty over the areas
and waters concerned. The Soviet Member's insistent
reference to "Soviet territorial waters" in this
correspondence strongly suggests that the movements of
Japanese fishing vessels outside the authorized fishing
area in the vicinity of islands now under Soviet occupation
have offered the Soviet Government a convenient opportunity
to advance a territorial claim, perhaps with the expectation
that out of correspondence on the fishing boat problem,
General Headquarters will give direct or tacit acknowledge-
ment.

There is also considerable evidence that the
Soviet Government nas already incorporated southern
Sakhalin, the Kuril Islands, and the Habomai-Shikotan
area into the Soviet Union South Sakhalin and the Kuril
Islands were reportedly made an administrative subdivision
of the Khabarovsk District by an ordinance issued by the
Presidium Executive Committee on February 20, 1946.
Reports from repatriates also indicate that all place
names have been given Russian designations, and that in
the spring of l946 a Soviet civil administration replaced.
the initial military government in southern Sakhalin,
the Kuril Islands, and the Habomai-Shikotan Group. On
February 25, 1947, the Soviet Union Presidium reportedly
adopted regulations making all Sakhalin and the Kuril
Islands a separate administrative area distinct from the
Khabarovsk District These reports, combined with formal
claims to territorial sovereignty noted above, plainly
indicate that t he Soviet Government intends to regard these
areas as permanent, integral parts of the Soviet Union.
II
Basis of Present Soviet Position in
Southern Sakhalin, the Kuril Islands,
and the Habomai-Shikotan Group
The basis of the present Soviet position in southern
Sakhalin and the Kuril Islands, but not in the Habomai
Group and Shikotan Island, is the Three Power Agreement
signed at Yalta on February 11, 1945, by Premier Stalin,
2
President Roosevelt, and Prime Minister Churchill. .This
agreement provides that among the "former rights of Russia
violated by the treacherous attack of Japan in l904 ...
the southern part of Sakhalin as well as the islends
adjacent to it shall be returned to the Soviet Union";.
and that, additionally, "the Kuril islands shall be
handed over to the Soviet Union." With the Soviet Union's
entry into the war against Japan on August 8, 1945, Soviet
forces immediately began the occupation of southern
Sakhalin, the Kuril Islands, and the Habomai-Shikotan
area. No part of the Yalta Agreement or any subsequent
agreements, however, specifically provided for the Soviet
occupation of these territories.
General Order No. 1 of September 2, 1945 (the day of
Japan's formal surrender), issued to the Japanese Govern-
ment by the Supreme Commander, directed that Japanese
forces in "Manchuria, Korea North of 38 degrees North
latitude, Karafuto, and the Kurile Islands shall surrender
to the Commander-in-Chief of Soviet Forces in the Far
East." General Order No. 1 makes no specific mention of
the Habomai Islands or Shikotan Island, and there is no
indication as to whether they were regarded as part of
the Kuril chain or as among the so-called "minor islands"
adjacent to the four main islands of Japan.
According
SECRET

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
. June 27; 1949.
-4-
According to Japanese sources, the surrender of
Japanese units in the above-cited areas to Soviet
forces was already taking place by virtue of an agreement,
reached on August 19, 1945, between the Chief of Staff
of the Japanese Kwantung Army in Manchuria and the
Commander-in-Chief of Soviet Forces in the Far East.
This agreement provided for the local cessation of
hostilities in Manchuria and the surrender of arms. At
the end of the war the Hokkaido-Kuril-Habomai-Shikotan
area was defended by the Fifth Area Army with headquarters
in Hokkaido. The northern Kuril Islands (from Shimushu
to Uruppu) were under the 9lst Division with headquarters
on Paramushiru Island; the southern Kuril Islands
(Etorofu and Kunashiri), the Habomai Group, and Shikotan
Island were defended by the 89th Division with headquarters
on Etorofu In view of the Soviet-Kwantung Army surrender
agreement of August 19. the Commanding General of the
Fifth Area Army ordered the 9lst Division in the northern
Kuril Islands to cease hostilities and surrender its arms
to the Soviet forces The northern Kuril Islands were
then being rapidly occupied by Soviet forces whioh
continued to move down the chain into the southern Kuril
Islands under the jurisdiction of the 89th Division.
On August 31 the Commanding General of the Fifth Area
Army placed the 89th Division under the comrand of the
9lst Division in order to facilitate the surrender of
the southern Kuril Islands into which Soviet foroes
were then pouring. Before the order could be executed,
however, the entire Kuril chain, including Kunashiri,
as well as the Habomai Group and Shikotan Island, were
completely in Soviet hands General Order No. 1 of
September 2, therefore, did not actually effeot the
surrender of Japanese forces in the Kuril Islands. Before
it was issued the Japanese in the Kuril Islands as well
as in the Habomai-Shikotan area had surrendered to the
Soviet forces on the basis of the August 19 agreement.
While providing a basis, or accord, for the Soviet
military occupation of the Kuril Islands and southern
Sakhalin, neither the Yalta Agreement nor any subsequent
understanding entitles the Soviet Union to territorial
sovereignty over these areas At most, the Yalta Agree-
ment must be regarded as a preliminary understanding
among the Soviet Union, the United States, and Great
Britain with respect to the future disposition of oertain
parts of the Japanese Empire, The Soviet Union, therefore,
cannot claim territorial sovereignty in southern Sakhalin
and the Kuril Islands any more than the United States
can claim territorial soveroignty over the Ryukyu Islands,
or China over Formosa. Southern Sakhalin, and the Kuril
Islands, like the Ryukyu Islands and Formosa, were
integral parts of the Japanese Empire, their Japanese
territorial status having been previously recognisedeby
the Powers, including the Soviet Union, the Unitua St
and China, in various treaties as well as in diplomatia
and general usage. What the Soviet Union do
present enjoy in southern Sakhalin and. the Kuril Islands
is administrative control by virtu of military ocoupation.
Unlike
SECRET

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Unlike southern Salhalin and Formosa which were
integral parts of the Japanese Empire but in the form
of colonial possessions with distinct ∞lonial administra-
tions, the Kuril Islands, like the Ryukyu Islands, were
administratively and politically, integral parts of
Metropolitan Japan. The Ryukyu Isl ands constituted one
of Japan's home prefectures. The Kuril Islands were an
integral part of the Hokkaido administration, forming
districts of one of the gun, or counties, which make
up the Nemuro Shicho, or Nemuro Branch Administration.
Notwithstanding the distinction between parts of
the Japanese Empire and integral parts of the Japanese
home islands (Metropolitan Japan), it is clear that
Sakhalin, the Kuril Islands, the Ryukyu Islands, and
Formosa were internationally recognized as territories
over which Japan enjoyed full and undisputed sovereignty.
From the standpoint of accepted international law and
procedure, therefore, any transfer of sovereignty over
these territories must be upon the basis of a formal
treaty or instrument to which Japan, as the party
relinquishing sovereignty, is a signatory. The problem
of Korea, however, appears to represent a distinct
exception to this generalization While Korea was
also an integral part of the Japanese Empire, it. was
also once an independent, sovereign state and was so
recognized by the Powers, incl uding Japan, in formal
treaties and agreements.' Its postwar detachment from
Japan does not, therefore, appear to be a territorial
transfer, but a restoration of sovereignty, the effecting
of which would not necessarily require formal acknowledg-
ment by Japan in a treaty or other international instrument.
III. Other Allied Policy Statements Concening
Japan's Territorial Limits
The Yalta Agreement does not specifically define
the geographical limits of the term "Kurile Islands"
used therein. Other declarations end policy documents
concerning the territorial limits of Japan are equally
general in so far as the so-called minor outlying islands
are concerned.
The Cairo Declaration of December 1, 1943, states
that "Japan shall be stripped of all the islands of'the
Pacific which she has seized or occupied since the
beginning of the First World War in 19l4, and that all
territories Japan has stolen from the Chinese such as
Manchuria, Formosa, and the Pescadores, shall be restored
to the Republic of China. Japan shall also be expelled
from all other territories which she has taken by
violence and greed." It is difficult to envisage
application
SECRET
1/ See Administrative Subdivisions of Japan, Department
of State, August, 1946.

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.98)
2.110..410
Jun 27, 1949.
-6-
application of the Cairo Declaration's territorial
provisions to the Kuril Islands They were acquired by
Japan long before the first World War, they were not
"stolen" from China (or anyone else), and the manner in
which Japan acquired the islands by the Shimoda and
Saint Petersburg treaties of 1855 and 1875, respectively,
hardly suggests that Japan secured this territory by
"violence and greed"
The Potsdam Declaration of July 26, 1945, however,
somewhat elaborates the territorial provisions outlined
at Cairo, with the statement that "the terms of the Cairo
Declaration shall be carried out and Japanese sovereignty
shall be limited to the islands of Honshu, Hokkaido,
Kyushu, Shikoku and such minor islands as we determine.
The Instrument of Surrender, signed on September 2, 1945,
makes no mention of territorial limitations, but the
United States Initial Post-Surrender Policy for Japan,
prepared jointly by the State, War, and Navy Departments
on August 29, 1945, restates the provisions of the Potsdam
Declaration to the effect that "Japan's sovereignty will
be limited to the islands of Honshu, Hokkaido, Kyushu,
Shikoku and such minor outlying islands as may be determined."
More specific is the Basic Initial Post-Sur render Directive
to SCAP for the Occupation a nd Control of Japan, prepared
by the Joint Chiefs of Staff (JCS Directive No. 18), dated
November 8, 1945. which defines Japan as "the four main
islands of Japan Hokkaido (Yezo), Honshu, Kyushu, and
Shikoku and about 1 Ooo smaller adjacent islands including
the Tsushima Islands "
JCS Directive No. 18 (also known as JCS 1380/15)
specifically provides in Section 4, d for the separation
of Japan from certain outlying areas.
"You /the Supreme Commander/will take appro-
priate steps in Japan to effect the complete
governmental and administrative separation
from Japan of (1) all Pacific islands which
she has seized or occupied under mandate or
otherwise since the beginning of the World
War in 1914, (2) Manchuria, Formosa, and
the Pescadores, (3) Korea, (4) Karafuto, and
(5) such other territories as may be specified
in future directives"
The implementation of this provision of JCS
Directive No. 18 was carried out by a General Headquarters
directive to the Japanese Government of January 29, 1946
(SCAPIN 677), whereby the Japanese Government was
directed "to cease exercising, or attempting to exercise,
governmental or administrative authority over any area
outside

A further paragraph of SCAPIN 677 excluded all Pacific
islands seized or occupied under mandate or otherwise
by Japan since the beginning of the World War in 1914,
Manchuria, Formosa, and the Pescadores, Korea, and

used in SCAPIN 677 is to apply to all future directives
and communications from General Headquarters, but "noth-
ing in this directive shall be construed as an indication
of Allied policy relating to the ultimate determination
of the minor islands referred to in Article 8 of the
Potsdam Declaration " This qualification in SCAPIN 677
does not apply, of course, to the territories enumerated
as being outside the directive's definition of Japan,
since Article 8 of the Potsdam Declaration is concerned
solely with the limiting of Japanese sovereignty to the
four main islands "and such minor islands as we determine".
It will at once be noted that SCAPIN 677 provides
a much more detailed and precise delimitation of Japan
than any definition found in previous documents, includ-
ing JCS Directive No 18 The detailed delimitation
defined in SCAPIN 677 to implement Section 4, d of JCS
Directive No. 18 was formulated in the Government Seotion
of General Headquarters, and the Habomai Islands and
Shikotan Island are for the first time specifically
mentioned in a document dealing with the boundaries of
Japan。

It would appear that in drafting SCAPIN 677 the

of Section 4, d of JC3 Directive No. 18 The terms of

implementation wouid require a more detailed definition

that JCS Directive No 18 mentioned neither the Kuril

Islands were in effect pledged to the Soviet Union by
the Yalta Agreement and were under Soviet occupation at the
time SCAPIN 677 was drafted, any practical implementation

mention of the Kuril Islends. The Habomai-Shikotan area,
however. was not mentioned in the Yalta Agreement, nor,
in fact hes itn er been mentioned in any international
instrument however far back in history, having always
been regarded and accepted as an integral part of Japan.
The only consideration favoring specific mention of this
area in SCAPIN 677 was its unilateral if not unauthorized
occupation by Soviet forces By specifically citing
the Habomai-Shikotan area, however, SCAPIN 677 tends
substantially to endorse if not validate the Soviet
position there It is especially significant that SCAPIN 677
does not describe the Habomai Islands and Shikotan Island
as an integral part of the Kuril archipelago. . On the
contrary, they are specifically enumerated along with
the Kurii Islands. and from the wording of the text of
SCAPIN 677 it is difficult to avoid the implication
that the Soviet Union is thereby recognized as enjoying
a position in both the Kuril Islands and the Habomai-
Shikotan area as two distinct regions. While the Yalta
Agreement "handed over" the Kuril Islands to the Soviet
Union, it may be said that SCAPIN 677 in effect "handed
over" the Habomai Group and Shikotan Island, and must,
therefore, be regarded as a kind of addendum to the
provisions of the Yalta agreement
It may be argued that practical considerations at
the time SCAPIN 677 was drafted precluded any other
course of action, especially the fact that Soviet forces
were actually in possession of the Habomai-Shikotan
area. Nevertheless, if there was ever a more opportune
time to attempt to clarify the status of the Habomai-
Shikotan islands, it was certainly before General Head-
quarters undertook by SCAPIN 677 to effect the governmental
and administrative separation of this territory from
Japan.

IV
Establishment of a Japanese Fishing Area
By SCAPIN 1033 of June 22, 1946 (as amended) the
Supreme Commander established an area embracing waters
around the Japanese islands in which Japanese fishing
and whaling are authorized The establishment of this
area is, of course, a SCAP administrative matter and
is not legally definitive of the territorial sovereignty
of Japan, although undoubtedly it will be a factor in
the Soviet claim to the entire Kuril chain as well as
to the Habomai Group and Shikotan Island.
The limit of the fishing area in the vininity
of the Kuril Islands was established at a line running
three miles off the eastern coast of Hokkaido to a
point off Cape Nosapru, and thence passing through a
point

V
Japanese Claims to the Kuril Islands
It wouli appear that those who participated in
the Yalta Ccnference were either ignorant of or completely
ignored the historical basis of Japan's position in the Kuril
Islands, and, accordingly. readily consented to the Soviet
demand that this territory be "handed over."
From at least the beginning of the l7th century,
the Japanese regarded the Kuril Islands (or what they
knew of the archipelago) and to a lesser extent Sakhalin
as parts of the northern Japanese district of Ezo (the early
name for Hokkaido) which the Tokugawa Shogunate in 1603
made the feudal fief of the Matsumae family.
The Kuril Islands first became vaguely known to
Europeans as a result of their "discovery" by the Dut ch
navigator Maarten GerritszooL a'Vries in 1643. His
crude map shows only Kunashiri ("State Land") and part of
Etorofu
SECRET

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.98)
June 27, 1949.
-11-
torofu ("Compagnies Land") as lying off the eastorn
coast of "Jeso" (Ezo, or Hokkaido).
The first Japanese map attempting to depict the
Kuril Islands is the famous Shoho Map, made in the firt
year of the Shot mere,  r 1644. Prepared on orders of
the Tokugawa Shogunate, the wap shows the entire area
of Japan, being a compilation of individual regional maps
prepared by the various feudal lords The section covering
northernmost Japan was therefore presumably made by.the
Matsumae daimyo to show the Matsumae domain. Ezo, or
Hokkaido, Is depicted in an exceedingly distorted manner;
to the North of Ezo is a smeller island (Sakhalin) and to
the northeast are over thirty shs ll islands, most of which
are named, representing the Kuril archipelago.
The so-called !o ani Map, prepared by Tokunai
Mogami and submitted to the Shogunate in 1791 with a
report on the custons and manner of Ezo, gives a fairly
accurate delineation of the Kuril Islands and the Habomai-
Shikotan group This  depenled to a considerable
extent on Russian sources for data on the Kuril Islands
north of Urupru, but Etorofu. Kunashiri, and the Habomai-
Shikotan area were drawn on the basis of extensive surveys
ofeEzo nd viei pity conducted on orders of the Shogunate
in the years 1785-1786
There are a number of other Japanese maps of this
area made before the l9th century, but in all maps and
chronicles of the period the Kurii Islands are elther
indicated directly or by implicution as being a part of
the Matsumae domain in Ezo
The first reported appearance of Russians in the Kuril
archipelago occurred about 171l-1713 when an exploring
party entered the island chain from Kamchatka. By the
end of the l8th century the Russians had extensively
explored the archipelago as far south as Uruppu where a small
settlement was established  The Russian advance alarmed
the Japanese who desputchel several expeditions toward
the end of the l8th century to investigate Russian aotivities,
make surveys, and establish markers proclaiming the islands
Japanese territory. As a further measure, the Shogunate
terminated the Matsumae administration of the Kuril Islands
in 1799 and assumed direct control over the territory.
In l8ol the Shogunate sent an expedition to Uruppu to
demand that the Russian settlement be withdrawn. The
Russians complied with this demand in l805 and thereafter
there appears to have been a kind of unwritten understand-
ing whereby the Uruppu Channel (between Uruppu and Shimushiru
islands) served as the boundary between the Russian and
Japanese spheres of influence in the Kuril Islands.
Before the Shogunate assumed direot administration
of the Kuril Islands in 1799, they had been universally
regarded in Japan as the private holdings of the Mataumae
family
SEORET

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.98)
-:-pe cch no: 416,
June 27, 1 19.
-13-
family which had enjoyed a feudal suzerainty over
Ezo as a result of having received the territory as a.
fief at the beginning of the 17th century. During the
first half of the 19th century, although the Tokugawa
Shogunate with its feudal basis of administration continued
to govern Japan, the Kuril Islands began to acquire a
more modern political significance with plain implications
of western concepts of national ownership and sovereignty.
This was occasioned in part by the transfer of the
territory frc clal (fcudal) administrtion to shogunal.
(netional) administrstion nd in part by the confliot
between Russian und Juranese interests in the islands
and subse ent negotiations on the subject which at
once gave the Kuril Tslands an international signiricanoe.
It is interesting to ote therefore that the more
accurate maps of the Ezo, J khalin, and the Kuril areas
in the first hnlf of tle lits century acquired an
unmistakable politicil character with distinot colors
employed to indicate national ownership or sovereignty.
One of the best maps of this new category is entitled
Kaisho Ezc Zenzu which it is significant to note means
"The Revised Map of Al! Ezo" The map was drawn by
Ki Toshima on the basis of Japanese surveys and available
information from Russin and other foreign souroes. It
was published by  pck printing in Edo (Tokyo) in
the 7th year of the kaei era, or 1854. A copy of this
map is in the undersigned's possession.
"The Revised Map of All zo" depicts Hokkaido,
the Kuril Islands, and Sakhalin Ezo is classified as
three areas The southern part of the island now known
as Hokkaido is labeled Ezo; the northern part, Nishi (West)
Ezo; the eastern part, Higashi (East) Ezo; while the
1sland of Sakhalin is marked Kita (North) Ezo. In an
explanatory note, it is stated that although there are
various names for Sakhalin. it is referred to as Kita Eso.
From the standpoint of coloring, the island now known as
Hokkaido, Sakhalin, Uruppu. Etorofu, and Kunashiri in the
Furil Islands, and Shikotan and the Habonai Islanls huve a
yellow border, indicating Japanese territory. The othor
Kuril Islands from Shimushiru northward have a green border,
indicating Russian territory.
The undersigned has in his possession a number of
other woodblock printed maps published in this period which,
while not so accurate from the cartographlo standpoint,
are of interest as examples of the extent to whioh the
Kuril Islands had acquired a modern political signifioance
to the Japanese

After the Treaty of Shimoda there was a marked
increase in Russian activity in Sakhalin, and the Japanese
Government decided that it was advisable to establish
a definite delimitation of the Russian and Japanese
positions. After a long period of negotiations a general
settlement was made by the Russo-Japanese Treaty of
Saint Petersburg, signed on May 7. 1875 This treaty
is officially known as the Treaty for the Exchange of
the Island of Sakhalin with the Group of the Kuril
Islands (Traite d'Echange de 1'ile de Sakhaline Contre
le Groupe des Iles Kouriles) By Article 1, Japan
ceded to Russia its rights and interests in Sakhalin,
and by Article 2, Russia ceded to Japan the portion of
the Kuril Islands then in Russia's possession, enumerated
as eighteen islands between Shimushu, at the extreme
northern tip of the chain, to Uruppu, inclusive. By
this treaty, the Russo-Japanese boundary in the Kuril
area was established as the straits between Cape Lopatka
on Kamchatka and Shimushu Island
The Saint Petersburg Treaty thus gave Japan full
sovereignty over the Russian portion of the Kuril Islands,
as rreviously recognized by the Treaty of Shimoda, making
the entire Kuril archipelago Japanese territory. It
should be noted that the Saint Petersburg Treaty was
concerned only with Uruppu and the Kuril Islands north
thereof. The erroneous statement frequently appears
th-t Japan, by the Saint Petersburg Treaty of 1875,
exchanged her interests in Sakhalin for all the Kuril
Islands. Like the Treaty of Shimoda, the xchunge agree-
ment of 1875 also made no reference to the Hapomai Islands
or Shikotan Island。
As a result of the Russo-Japanese War (1904-1905),
Japanese forces invaded and occupied Sakhalin. At the
Portsmouth Peace Conference,  denanded that Russia
cede to Japan the entire isiand, but a compromise
settlement was finally incorporated in Article 9 of the
Portsmouth Treaty of September 5, 1905, by which Japan
received from Russia the southern half of Sakhalin with
the 50th degree of north latitude serving as the boundary
between the two countries. The Kuril Islands, let alone
the Habomai-Shikotan area, was not discussed at the
Portsmouth Peace Conference.
VII. The Validity of the Yalta Agreement
Viewed from the historical stendpoint, the Soviet
Union has no valid claim to the Kuril Islands, except,
of course, as a war prize for the Soviet Union's belated
and limited participation in the war against Japan. On
this basis, however, the Soviet Union would be equally
entitled to claim any other part of Japan as a war
prize, such as Hokkaido.
The
SECRET

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
D.s atch no.: 416,
2719
SzOnLT
-17-
The Soviet claim to southern Sakhalin, also
endorsed by the Yalta Agreement, has some validity
in that Japan had once recognized, by the Saint Petersburg
Treaty of i875, full Russian sovereignty over the entire
island, but had later, as a result of the Russo-Japanese
War, received the southern half of the island on the basis
cf what might be called a "war prize" claim. The Kuril
Islands, however, afford no such considerations for
governing their disposition
The Habomai Islands and Shikoten Island were not
specifically mentioned in the Yalta Agreement. While a
part of the Kuril Islands and Sakhalin were at various
times the subjects of conflicting claims between Japan
and Russia and were dealt with in formal treaties between
the two countries, at no time were the Habomai Islands
and Shikotan Island ever the subjects of international
dispute or discussion These islands have always been
regarded and recognized as integral parts of Metropolitan
Japan.
The present Soviet position in the Habomai Islands
and Shikotan Island stems from the failure to make a
clear and accurate definition of the Kuril Islands at the
time the Yalta Agreement was concluded, the unilateral
occupation of the Habomai-Shikotan area by Soviet forces
at the end of the war, and the tacit endorsement of
Soviet occupation afforded by General Headquarters in
SCAPIN 677 and SCAPIN 1053. The Habomai-Shikotan area
was apparently never mentioned in the discussions at
Yalta, and the Soviets were  ly permitted thereby
to have the impression that we considered these islands
a part of the Kuril chain, geographically and administratively.
When Japan surrendered, Soviet forces lost no time in
occupying these islands, aided by the fact, of course,
that no other Allied force was thel. to cupy them first.
They have thus been acquired by the Soviet Union largely
through default
VIII
Future Considerations
By virtue of the loose language of the Yalta
Agreement, along with the Soviet Union's rapid occupation
of the Kuril Islands, the Habomai Group, and Shikotan
Island in the last days of the war, the Soviet Government
is able to advance substantial territorial claims to these
areas which are difficult to contest Furthermore, the
Soviet Government is now in the adventageous position to
point, if necessity arises, to what amounts to tacit
recognition of the Soviet position in these islands by
measures which in effect supplement end fortify the terms
of the Yalta Agreement, namely, SCAPIN 677 and SCAPIN
1033. Nevertheless, in any future discussion of this
subject, such as may take place at a peace conference,
it would be well to take into careful account the foliowing
considerations:
SECRET
1. Historical

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
tch no. 416,
T 27, 1019.
-18-
1. Historical Position of the Kuril Islands. By the
Saint Petersburg Treaty of 1875, in exchange for Japan's
relinquishment of her claims to and interests in Sakhalin,
Russia relinquished certain islands; but these were not
the entire chain from Kamchatka to Hokkaido, because the
entire chain was not in Russia's possession. The two
southern islands of Kunashiri and Etorofu have always in
historical times belonged to Japan, a situation which
Russia formally recognized as early as the Shimoda Treaty
of 1855 By the Saint Petersburg Treaty, Russia merely
relinquished what was then recognized as the Russian
portion of the chuin, namely, Uruppu and the islands
north thereof
2. Historical Position of the Habomai Group and
Shikotan These islends have always been regarded and
recognized as integral parts of Japan, and their status
has never been the subject of discussion or dispute between
Japan and eny other country The only instruments of
an international character dealing directly with this
Warea are SCAPIN 677 and SCAPIN 1033, both of which,
however, should be regarded as purely SCAP administrative
measures having no legal bearing on the ultimate disposition
of these islands.
3. The Problem of an Effective Boundary. If the
Soviet Union is permitted to remain in permanent possession
of the entire Kuril chain from Kamchatka to Hokkaido and
the Habomai Group and Shikotan Island, the boundary between
Japan's nosition in Hokkaido and the Soviet position in
the Kuril-Iubomai area will present a difficult problem.
The narrow waters separating Hokkaido from the Kuril
Islands and the Habomai Group bear considerable traffic
during the fishing season when there are frequently
dense fogs in this srea Such a situation will unguestion-
ably lead to repeated violations of Soviet-held waters
by Japanese vessels and violations of Japanese waters
by-Soviet craft From a practicel standpoint, therefore,
a boundary line running between Hokkaido and Kunashiri
Island and thence through the narrow Goyomai Straits
between Cape Nosappu and Kaigara-jima does not offer a
satisfactory boundary。
4. Economic Considerations. Heretofore, the
important Hokkaido fisheries have depended to a considerable
extent upon the waters of the Kuril Islands, the Habomai
Group, and Shikotan. From the Habomai area alone was
produced almost half of Hokkaido's edible seaweed which
in turn amounted to about 97 percent of Japan's total
production. Furthermore, other fisheries, particularly
crab, in the Hokkaido region are primarily dependent
upon the waters between the Habomai-Shikotan group and
Kunashiri
SECRET

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.97)
1e.c no..41o,.
June 27, 1940.
SECRET
-19-
Kunashiri Island. The loss of these islands represents
an exceedingly critical restriction on Japan's already
overtaxed facilities for producing foodstuffs. From
pre-war experience with the Soviet-Japanese fishing
conventions, by which Japanese were permitted to fish
in Siberian waters, it is very unlikely that Japan in the
foreseeable future will be able to acquire fishing rights
from the Soviet Union in the Kuril Islands, the Habomai
Group, Shikotan, or elsewhere
5 Strategic Considerations Soviet possession of
Kunashiri Island and the Habomai Islands brings the
ominous power of the Soviet Union within exceedingly
close proximity to Metropolitan Japan That the Japanese
must regard such close proximity to their disarmed homeland
a grave liability is only natural
In view of these important historical, economic,
and strategic considerations, Japan is fuliy justified
in expecting a reasonable territorial ustment in
this area. It is believed that such a readjustment should
restore to Japan those territories which have always
been recognized as integral parts of the Japanese home
islands and which have never heretofore been the subject
of dispute, namely, the two southernnost Kuril Islands
of Etorofu and Kunashiri, the Habonai Islands, and
Shikotan Island Such a readjustment could be of no
conceivable loss or burden to the Soviet Union, either
economically or strategically The territory involved
consists of only 1,868 69 square mile. 1,8 0.11 square
miles represented by Kunashiri and Etororu, nd only
38 58 square miles represented by the Habomai Group and
Shikotan The restoration of this territory to Japan,
however, would go far to rehabilitate the vital Hokkaido
fisheries, eliminate a real strategic hazard to Japan,
and provide a far more satisfactory political boundary
betw en Soviet and Japanese territories. Instead of
the tortuous "MecArthur Line" now running through the
narrow, fog-bound Nenuro, Kokke, and Goymai channels,
the boundary would be at the center of the Etorofu Straits,
the deep water channel, free from dangerous tides and
currents, which separates Etorofu and Uruppu islands, with
a width of 22 miles at its narrowest point.

Subject: Directive Authorizing Japanese Fishery Inspection System.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no. 92
of February 12, 1949, forwarding a plan of the Natural Resources
Section of lieadquarters for a Japanese fishery patrol and the
comments of this Mission on that plan. In that connection there
1/
is enclosed a copy of a directive shortly to be issued to the
Japanese Government aut horizing the establishment of a fishery
894, 60.28
inspection systen substantially similar to the plan enclosed in
the despatch under reference. The enclosed directive, which it
is expected will be issued to the Japanese Government in a few
days, incorporates the suggestions made earlier by this Mission
to the effect that the word "patrol" should be avoided, that the
authority of the inspection vessels should be clearly stated,
and that the boundary of the area to be patrolled should be de-
fined.
The Note for Record which is appended to the enclosed
directive and which contains classified information, indicates
that eleven vessels are to be used by the Fisheries Agency of
the Japanese Government and, as is stated in the directive, are to
operate westward somewhat beyond the authorized area in the
Yellow andEast China Seas and southward and eastward considerably
beyond the authorized fishing area. The Note for Record also
estimates that it will require an appropriation of about 150,000,000
to finance the inspection system. It is understood that the
Economic and Scientific Section of Headquarters, after considerable
deliberation, authorized this expenditure.

Natural Resources Section officials indicate their hope that

SCAPIN
MEMORANDUM FOR: THE JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
SUBJECT:
Japanese Fishery Inspection System
1. Reference:
a. Memorandum for Japanese Government from General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, AG
800.217 (22 Jun 48)NR, SCAPIN 1033, subject, "Area Authorized
for Japanese Fishing and Whaling," 22 June 1946, and SCAPIN
1033/1, 23 December 1948.
b. Memorandum to General Headquarters, Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers, from the Japanese Government,
C.L.C.0. No. 2852 (2P), subject, "Measures to Prevent Japanese
Fishing Boats from Trespassing Upon Area Outside Boundary of
Authorized Area," 11 August 1948.
2. The Japanese Government is authorized to establish a
fisheries inspection system as proposed by Memorandum referred
to in paragraph 1 b above, subject to the modifications listed
below:
a. The purpose of the inspection system authorized by
this Memorandum is to enforce the provisions of Memorandum for
the Japanese Government, SCAPIN 1033, and any subsequent amend-
ments or modifications thereof, as well as other pertinent
fisheries regulations. Inspection vessels will be responsible
for assisting in the enforcement of instructions issued by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and of laws and regulations
of the Japanese Government in regard to Japanese fishery operations.
b. Available vessels of the type and size suitable for
conducting inspection duties will be put into immediate operation
and provisions will be made to increase the number of vessels used
if the need for such is demonstrated to be necessary to insure
effective enforcement.
c. Inspection vessels are authorized to operate within
the area bounded as follows: From a point midway between Nosappu
Misaki and Kaigara Jima at approximately 43023'14" North Latitude,
145050'30

Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No.
424, July 1, 1949.
-2-
145050'30" East Longitude; to 430North Latitude, 146030' East
Longitude; thence to 450North Latitude, 165oEast Longitude;
thence east along the 450 parallel to the 180th meridian; thence
south along the 180th meridian to l0oNorth Latitude; thence west
along the 10oNorth parallel to 132oEast Longitude; thence to 220
North Latitude, 1260East Longitude; thence to 24oNorth Latitude,
122030' East Longitude; thence north to 34oNorth Latitude, 122030'
East Longitude; thence east to 340North Latitude, 128040' East
Longitude; thence to 400North Latitude, 1350East Longitude; thence
45030' North Latitude, 1400East Longitude; thence east to 45030'
North Latitude; 1450East Longitude rounding Soya Misaki at a
distance of three (3) miles from shore; south along the 145th meridian
to a point three (3) miles off the coast of Hokkaido; thence along a
line three (3) miles off the coast of Hokkaido rounding Shiretoko
Saki and following a mid-channel course through the Nemuro Kaikyo to
a point 43026'17" North Latitude, 145048'03" East Longitude; thence
in a south-easterly direction to the starting point midway between
Nosappu Misaki and Kaigara Jima.
d. Japanese inspection vessels shall not approach closer
than ten (lo) miles to the coast of any foreign country or its island
possessions within the area defined in the preceding paragraph.
e. The modified International E instead of the Japanese
flag shall be used to mark inspection vessels.
f. Inspection vessels will not engage in fishing operations
of any kind.
g. Inspection vessels shall obtain authorization for each
voyage from Commander, United States Naval Activities, Japan.
h. Inspection vessels will not be vested with police
powers.
3. The Japanese Government shall take immediate action to
carry out all phases of proposed plan as modified in paragraph 2,
above.
4. Direct communication between Natural Resources Section,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and
the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, concerning matters within
the scope of this memorandum, is authorized.
5. This authorization does not establish a precedent for the
operation of inspection vessels in any other area for any subsequent
period of time; nor is it an expression of Allied policy relative to
the ultimate

FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:
CONF IDENTIAL
NOTE FOR RECORD:
1. SCAPIN 1033, 22 Jun 46 and SCAPIN 1033/1, 23 Dec 48,
define the area in which Japanese fishing boats are authorized
to carry on fishing operations. Imperial Ordinance 3ll, 11 Jun
46, guides the Japanese in enforcing SCAPINs and prosecuting
violations.
2. Between 30 Dec 46 and 20 Oct 48 seventy-eight instances
of Japanese boats fishing outside the authorized area were reported.
Information substantiating violations was sufficient to obtain
convictions in Japanese courts in only three of these instances.
3. In a memorandum dated 8 May 48 the Chinese Mission informed
DS that it was directed by its home Government to notify the Japa-
nese Government through General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, that the Chinese Government would have to take
appropriate measures unless such violations were effectively checked.
4. In compliance with instructions of the Chief, Fisheries
Division, NR, the Japanese Government on 13 Aug 48 submitted a plan
embracing all measures considered essential to the satisfactory dis-
charge of its responsibility to enforce SCAPIN 1033. The principal
proposals of this plan are:
a. Eleven vessels owned or to be chartered by the Fisheries
Agency are to be authorized to patrol Eastern China waters and the
southern and eastern boundaries of the authorized area. All patrol
vessels would be marked with a Japanese flag painted in the middle,
on both sides of the boat, and a white line around the ship.
b. All fishing boats are to be marked with their registra-
tion numbers at a place suitable to be seen from the upper air, in
addition to the mark on the bow prescribed by the Fishing Boat Registra-
tion Regulation.
c. Fishing vessels with radio equipment, including otter
trawlers and bull trawlers of over 50 gross tons and tuna boats over
75 gross tons, shall report their position every noon to the Fisheries
Agency. All boats of these types and sizes which do not have such
radio equipment must be so equipped within six months from the day
CONFIDENTIAL
approval

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No,
424, July 1, 1949.
-4-
approval is given the plan by SCAP. Any such vessel not so equipped
within six months will be restricted to operate in an area small
enough to reduce the possibility of violations. Preliminary con-
ferences on this subject between the Fisheries Agency and the Mini-
stry of Communications indicate that a satisfactory schedule of
time and radio frequencies can be arranged for this purpose.
d. Penalties for violation of SCAPIN 1033 will include:
(1) Confiscation of the catch or of an amount of money
equivalent to the price, will be imposed on the
operator. The estimated value of the catch is,
otter trawler ¥2,500,000, bull trawler ¥2,000,000,
tuna boat ¥1,000,000 to ¥2,300,000 depending on
size of boat.
(2)
Confiscation of fishing facilities (gear). The
current value of fishing facilities varies from
¥550,000 to ¥2,500,000 depending on type and
size of boat.
(3)
Sentencing of the captain of the boat to imprison-
ment with hard labor not to exceed 10 years, and
imposing a fine of not more than ¥75,000.
(4)
Administrative cancellation of vessel's license to
engage in fishing operations.
e. The fishing boat operators and associations undertake to:
(1)
Warn all persons suspected of violations and report
them to the Fisheries Agency.
(2)
Prepare posters for prevention of violations under
instructions of the Fisheries Agency and post them
in the pilot houses and crew spaces of all deep-sea
fishing ships and at fishing bases.
f. Permission is requested for the patrol boats to operate
in the area described. This includes a small area of water outside
of the authorized area in the Yellow and East China Seas and a con-
siderable area to the south and east of the authorized fishing area.
g. To carry out this plan will require an appropriation
of an estimated 150,000,000 to charter patrol vessels and cover the
expenses, and an allotment of about 113,000 kilograms of steel,
74,300 kilograms of copper, 36,000 kilograms of lead, 7,300 kilograms
of pig iron, and 2, 500 koku of lumber, for the manufacture and in-
stallation of 226 radio sets on 209 bull trawlers and 17 tuna boats
now without such sets.
5. The

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COIMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, presents its compliments to
the United Kingdom Liaison Mission in Japan and has the honor
to invite attention to the fact that, in order to protect the
interests of the Allied Powers (particularly those directly
concerned with Japanese fishing) and in order to contribute to
proper regulation of Japanese fishing activities, the Supreme
Commander is about to authorize the Japanese Government to es
tablish a fisheries inspection system. Inspection vessels,
which will be without police powers and will not engage in
fishing, will be permitted to operate within the area defined
by the authorization.
Enclosed is a copy of the Supreme Comnander's contemplated
instruction shortly to be issued to the Japanese Government
containing this authorization.
