=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 427 dated July 1, 1949, from U
"Japanese Fish Production and Distribution".
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMAND
Public Information Office
27 June 1949
Information Bulletin No. 7
OVERALL FISHERIES FRODUCTION.
SCAI's Natural Resources Section has estimated Japanese fisheries production
for 1950 at 7,011,000,000 pounds and 7,108,000,000 pounds for 1951 as compared
with 5,700,000,000 pounds in 1948 and an estimated 6,800,000,000 pounds this year.
actual production, it vas pointed out, may deviate considerably from the
estimates as the result of biological or oceanographic conditions. As in fisher-
ies in other parts of the world, the production of the Japanese fisheries, parti-
cularly some of the pelagic species, are featured by sharp fluctuations, the prin-
cipal causes of which are vriations in environmental conditions affecting spawn-
ing and survival of young a.i in oceanic conditions affecting the migrations and
availability of marketable fish.
The sardine and herring fisheries, which have been declining in recent years
are liable to such sharp fluctuations both in abundance and availability. The con-
ditions affecting the catch of these fish in particular may affect production sig-
nificantly for 1950 and 1951.
Since the surrender in 1945, shortages of materials such as nets, twine,
and rope have linited production seriously. Beginning in 1948 larger quantities
of materials were imported by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for the
Japanese fisheries, and in 1949 it is expected that the supplies of such materials
will about meet the minimum requirements for the present SCAP-authorized fishing
area.
By 1950 and l951 it is expected that there wil1 be no serious shortage of
materials, so that production will reach the maximum which can be obtained fron the
present area on a sustained yield basis. Further increases will depend on improve-
ments in sardines and herring, butter management of other fisheries to increase
their productivity, and expansion of aquiculture.
New ice manufacturing and ice storage plants have been constructed to in-
crease the over-all daily cutput of ice by 900 tons. This gain of about 6.5 per-
cent over 194s production will reduce the deficit in ice supply to the fishing
industry from about 350,000 tons in 1948 to about 30,000 tons in 1949.
The incentive for fishermen to use more care in handling fish at sea is in-
creased by the fact that a ptrict inspection system is being .established at all
major landing ports, and a  vision of tie link system for distribution of fuel
oil is being considered. As soon as the inspection standards and procedures are
firmly established, it is planned that no distribution of fuel oil will be made
in exchange for fish of substandard quality.
Marketing conditions nave improved owing to the increase in ice production
noted above, an increase in cold storage capacity, a marked improvement in rail
transportation facilities, and increased capacities in various phases of the fish
processing. industry.
The cold storage capacity in Japen is being increased by about 1,800 tons.
More than 1, 000 tons of this capacity expansion have been completed, and the remain-
der is expected to be constructed by December 1949. During 1948, 250 refrigeration
cars of 25-ton capacity were constructed and are in operation. The construction of
300 additional 15-ton capacity cars in 1949 is in the planning stage. If this
plan is finalized it ill greatly case bottlenecks existing in rail transportation.
The system used in the United States for accelerating the movement of per-
ishable products trains has been adopted by the Japanese. Reefer, cars containing
such perishables as fish are now moved on a fast schedule with a minimum of delay
at switching points. Consequently, fish shipments are arriving at their destina-
tions in much bettur condition. Also, a system has been established whereby all
rail cars used for the transport of foodstuffs are cleaned at regular intervals.
The cars are inspected frequently to insure that the procedure established is func-
tioning.
(more)
(over)

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
Increases in capacity of fish processing facilities have been re
the construction or rehabilitation of freezing and cold storage plant
plants; and, other processing establishments such es fish meal and f
This permits the routing of a larger portion of the catch to these industr
thus relieving to same extent the gluts of fresh fish distribution channels
have occurred in the past
Lesiglation in the forn of the Food Sanitation Act has been promulgated and
is being implemented. About 2, C00 inspectors have been assigned to major fish
receiving and shipping ports and ere being trained to grade fish according to its
end use.
Fish in prime condition is designated for transport to large ∞nsuming areas
which are distant from the point of origin; fish of lower quality is allocated
for consumption in local or nearby areas; and fish unfit for human consumption
is designated as fertilizer.
When this inspection system is firmly established and functioning properly
it may be expected that price schedules will be adopted to conform to the grades
of fish. This will serve to encourage the fish market operators to handle the
fish in a proper manner and take cognizance of sanitary requirements.
Facilities for aquatic research in Japan are more numerous and extensive
than in any other country, but research on fish population for the purpose of
determining the management regulations needed to maintain high yield is far be-
hind that in many other countries, both in quantity and quality. However, reor-
ganization of the governme, 1 research work has been initiated to provide for
better coordination and at: tion to conservation problems, and several research
programs on this subject are underway.
The department of the Japanese government having responsibility for fisher-
ies matters has been roorganised and advanced from the status of a bureau to that
of an agency. This step should result in more efficient administration and re-
search, inasmuch as one result of the reorganization is the creation of a Division
of Research. This division will coordinate and supervise all research on fisher-
ies problems done by the central governaent.
Cooperative programs for research on the herring and sardine fisheries,
which have declined greatly in recent years, have been undertaken by the central
government and some of the prefectural research institutions.

FISHING INDUSTRY TO REVAMP SETUP
30 Per Cent Readjustment
Plan to Be Carried Cut
in Dragnet Operation
In view of SCAP's rejection of the recent Japanese petition re-
questing the expansion of Japan's fisheries grcunds. the Agriculture
and Forestry Ministry has decided to stsictly live up to its present
fishing limits and te positively enferce varicus measures to maintein
her natural resources.
As the first step toward achieving this pupose, the Ministry has
resclved to carry out a 30 per cent reedjustment of the dragnet and traw?
industries now operating in the western seas.
The proposed readjustment was announced by the Merine Products Board
of the Agriculture and Forestry Ministry Monday, according to Kyodo.
The Gcvernment s read jus tment plan was slated to be discussed at a
four day meeting of the Japan Deep-Sea Dragnet Fishing Industxy Society
held at Shimonoseki Monday.

2 July 1949
INSPECTION STEVICE FOR FISHING FLEET
An inspection service designed to contain the Japanese fishing fleet within
the postwar, SCAF designated boundaries has received C. approval.
Specifications for the eleven ship fleet were proposed by the Japanese
governnent as were reconnendations for penalties to be applied to crews and owners
of boats found quilty of operating in unauthorized waters.
SCAF instruction to the Japanese government outlined the area within which
the Japanese fishing fleet may operate. Although this area contains rich fishing
resources, the authorized fishing grounds are smaller than the area Japanese
fishermen usually covered in prevar days.
Unarmed, the inspection fleet is without police power and may only report
on the activities of fishermen and fishing boats. In case the original inspection
flcet proves inadequate to the task, a later increase in size was authorized in
the original draft of the plan.
At present, approximately ons thousand trawlers fish the shallow waters
between Japan and the coast of the Asia mainland and an equal number of tuna
fishing ships work the sui ce of the deep water of the Facific west of the 165th
merridian. Radio as well  visual cbservation will be used to maintain sur-
veillance over this fleet. Each of the eleven inspection craft will be equipped
with radio and according to the provisions of the inspection progran, each fishing
craft operating offshore must also be squipped vith radio within six months
after the inspection system received SCAF approval. Minimum size of boats to
be allowed to operate offshorc has been set at 60 gross tons for trawlers and
75 gross tons for tuna fishing boats.
By means of triangulation using radio compass, two inspection ships can
establish the location of a single fishing boat. Single inspection craft oper-
ating near the border of the fishing grounds can also detemaine from the direc-
tion of the calls received whether  not the sender is outside the authorized
fishing area. Fishing ships will be required to report thcir positions each day.
Each inspection craft will fly the modified international E flag and each
will be marked so tihat it can be easily identified from the air. Inspection
ships may not approach closer than 10 miles to the coast of any foreign nation
which borders the fishing grounds and no inspection craft will be allowed to
participate in fishing operations.
The program proposed by the Japanese government approved by SCAP provides
the following maximum penalties as punishment for those who are found quilty of
infractions of the regulations.
The penalties will include:
Confiscation of fish catch or a fine equivalent to the cash value of the
catch; confiscation of fishing gear including nets, etc., but excluding the fish-
ing ship; imprisonment of no fishing ship caj.tain for not more than 10 years or
fine of not more than 75,000 yen, and administrative cancellation of the fishing.
vessel's right to fish.

PHARMACEU M1C L XFYRS ARRIVE
Five members of the Anerican Pharmaceutical Association who will review pro-
gress in pharmaceutical affairs. in Japan for SCAF's Fublic Health and Welfare
Section, arrived here by air.last night.
They are Dr. Glenn L. Jenkins, president of the American Pharriccutical
Association and Dean of the College of Pharmacy, Purdue University, LaFayette,
(more)

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.98)
Ind.; Dr. Hugh C. Muldoon, Dean of the College of Pharmacy Duqueane
Pittsburgh; Dr. Troy Dahiels, Dean of College of Pharmacy, University
ornia at Berkeley; Don Francke, chief pharmacist, University of Michig
at Ann Arbor; and F. Royce Franzoni, vice president of of the National
Board of Pharmaoy of Washington, D.C.
During their 30 day visit members of the Mission will confer with goyern
mental officials, education, professional and industrial leaders in pharmacy,
as well as visit various types of establishments concerned with these activities.
Later they will' tour Japan for 10 days, visiting Hiroshima, Fukuoka, Osaka,
Kyoto and Gifu.

EDUCATORS JOIN CIE
Two American educators, who worked with Japanese educators here last winter,
have returned to serve with SCAP's Civil Information and Education Section.
Dr. Luther W. Stalnak, Des Moines, on leave from Drake University where he
is Dean of the Cellege of  beral Arts nd Frofessor of Philosophy, has joined
the staff of the Civil Information and Education Section as advisor on social
sciences and huanities. He also is assisting Japanese government agencies con-
cerned with the simplification of the Japanese language. Dr. Stalnaker was a
member of the five-man U.S. Cultural Science Mission that last winter worked with
Japanese educators in the humanities and social sciences.
Miss Pdna Ambrose, Sringvale, Me., who was one of the U.S. specialists on.
the staff of the Institute for Educational Leadership held in Tokyo last winter,
has been assigned to the Elementary Education unit of CIE. Miss Ambrose taught
at the University of Main and Harvard's School of Education during sunmer ses-
sions.

MEHORANDUI: FOR: JAPTIESE GOVERNMENT
SUSJECT:
Japanese Whaling Operations in the Antarctic in
the 1949-1950 Season.
1. Reference is maae to:
a. Kemorandut for the Japanese Government from General
Heauquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, AG 800.217
(23 Nov 48)NR/Fi SCA.IN 1942, 23 November 1948, Subject, "Whaling
Industry."
b. Lenorandun! from the Japanese Government to General
Leadquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied rowers, MAF No.
76'49, dated 13 May 1949.
2. Japanese whaling operations in the ntarctic are hereby
authorized for the 1949-1950 season. Such operations wil1 be
conducted in accordance with the provisions of reference memo-
randum, l a, above.
3. Within thirty days of the receipt of this memorandum
the Ja anese Government will submit to the Supreme Commander
for the Allied rowers for approval a plan for the organization
and operation of an Antarctic whaling expedition consisting-of.
two flcets. The plan vill include a list of the names and gross
tonnages of the factory ships, catchers, and auxiliaries to be
used and the nanes of the personnel who will manage each fleet:
Subseguent to the approval of the plan or modification thereof,
any substitution of vessels or personnel for those named must
be approved by the Supreme Commander for the llied rowers.
4. Vessels participating in the whaling expedition will
comply with the following:
a. Each vessel vill be marked and operated in con-
formity with cirectives from the Administrator, Naval Shipping
Control Authority.
b. The master of each of the participating factory
shirs will maintain a duplicate of the ship's log which will be
available for inspection at all times and upon completion of the
expedition will be submitted, properly certified, to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.

c. During the voyage to an from the whalins area
no vessel will approach land nearer than 12 miles. The route to
be followed by each vessel will be supulied to the Supreme Com-
funder for the Allied rowers and the Commander, Naval Forces, Far
East, by the Japanese Governrent 15 days prior to the departure
of each vessel from Japan and fron the whaling area.
d. The naster of each of the participating factory
ships durin the periods enroute to and from the whaling area
will Torward daily radiograms to the Fisheries ency in Tokyo
listing the position of his ship at l200 hours local time on
the dute the radiogram is dispatched.
e. The mastcer of each of the participatins factory ships.
during the whaling operations will forward a daily radiogram to
the Fisheries Agency in Tokyo containing the followine information:

on the date the radiogram is dispatched.
(2)
Number and kind of whales processed during the

upon conclusion of wi alin operations, dispatch a radiogram to

of his ship from the whaling area.
5. Applications for freguencies for radio communications
by the whalin fleets vill be made to Civil Cormunications Section,
Goneral Headouarters, Suprene Comander for the Allied Fowers.

rovers when desired.

ultimate determination of nutional jurisdiction, international
boundarics, or fishing or whaling operations in any aree.

Subject: Seizures of Japanese Fishing Vessels by

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honór to refer to this Mission's
despatch no. 52 of January 25, 1949, concerning reports
of a Chinese law providing for the seizure of Japanese
m
fishing vessels outside the authorized fishing area
and enclosing a copy of a memorandum sent to the Chinese
Mission in Japan on January 24, 1949, by this Mission
in its capacity of Diplomatic Section, General Head-
quarters.
In a communication dated May 14, 1949, the Chinese
Mission explained that the measure authorizing seizure
of Japanese fishing vessels outside the authorized
fishing area was an Executive Order of the Chinese
Government issued on August 5, l948. The Chinese
Mission's communication attempted to justify this action
as being in conformity with a proposed policy decision
on fishing and aquatic industries in Japan forwarded to
the Steering Committee, Far Eastern Commission, at its
144th meeting on March 15, 1949 (SC 271 series). The
Chinese Mission's communication also claimed that China
has "traditional fishing interests" in the waters between
the western limits of the authorized fishing area and the
Chinese coast. A copy of the Chine se Mission's communi-
1/
cation of May 14 is enclosed.
On July 18 this Mission in its capacity of Diplomatic
Section, General Headquarters, sent a strong protest to
the Chinese Mission over seizures of Japanese fishing
vessels, the Chinese attempt to justify the Executive
Order
CONFIDENTIAL
DECLASSIFIED
NND780069
By.NNARS. Do1e 1OSRt179
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DO/R PILES WITHIN 14 DAYE, WITH A NOTATION OF ACTION TAKEN.

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatoh no.
553, August 13, 1949.
-GONFIDENTIAL
-2-
Order of August 5, 1948, on the basis of a Far Eastern
Commission working paper, and the claim of the right to
seize Japanese vessels on the high seas by virtue of
"traditional fishing interests" in the area. The
Chinese Mission was requested to inform the Chinese
Government of the Supreme Commander's concern over this
matter and of his desire that seizures of Japanese vessels
cease immediately. It was also requested that all vessels
seized be released and returned to Japan with their orews,
along with detailed reports of each incident in order
that the persons responsible might be brought to trial
in Japan in case the vessels violated the Supreme Commander's
directive by proceeding beyond the limits of the authorized
2/
fishing area. A copy of this communication is enclosed.
On Ju; 27, 1949, this Mission sent another strong
3/
protest to he Chinese Mission (copy enclosed) over the
seizure of Japanese vessels by Chinese naval patrols.
These fishing vessels have reportedly been armed and are
being used with their Japanese crews by the Chinese Navy
for military purposes in the current hostilities with
the Chinese Communists.
In addition to the four Japanese vessels seized by
Chinese Naval patrols, referred to in the communication
to the Chinese Mission of June 27, the Natural Resources
Section of General Headquarters has informed this Mission
that seven other Japanese vessels have been captured by
4/
Chinese patrols. A third protest, copy of which is enclosed,
was accordingly made to the Chinese Mission on August 8,
1949。
While this Mission doubts if concrete results can
be expected in the present circumstances from the despatch
of notes of protest to the Chinese Mission on this subject,
it is believed that such action is necessary as a matter
of form and for the record. In view of the Chinese
Government's insistent claim of the right to seize Japanese
fishing craft in waters between the western limits of
the authorized fishing area and the Chinese coast, and
since the Nationalist Government in any case probably
no longer exercises effective control over local authorities
in the areas where seizures have occurred, it is doubted
if effective action by the Chinese Government to prevent
further seizur es of Japanese fishing craft by Chinese
patrols is likely unless General Headquarters is prepared
to take appropriate countermeasures. This problem, is,
however, complicated by the fact that Soviet authorities
in the Sakhalin-Kuril Island area have in a similar manner
assumed the right to seize Japanese fishing vessels
reportedly found outside the authorized fishing area
(this Mission's despatch no. 34 of January 19, 1949).
It
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.98)
553, August 13, 1949。
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
It appears that Generel Headquarters is in a
disadvantageous position in dealing with this matter
so long as there is not an adequate patrol of the boundaries
of the authorized fishing area. The Natural Resources
Section has repeatedly warned the Japanese fishing authori-
ties of the necessity for strict compliance with the
provisions of the Supreme Commander's directive establish-
ing the fishing area (SCAPIN l033, as amended), and various
steps have been taken to deal with cases of proven
violation. While there is reason to believe that the
Japanese are being more circumspect in their observation
of the fishing regulations, there have been a number of
cases where there is little doubt that both Soviet and
Chinese authorities have seized Japanese craft within
the authorized fishin area.
The recent esta lishment of the Japanese Fisheries
Inspection Fleet for the purpose c. patrolling the
boundaries of the fishing area at critical points will
probably ameliorate this problem to some extent by
keeping Japanese vessels within the authorized area.
Japanese patrol craft, however, are unarmed and will
be in no position to deal with seizures within the fishing
area. Moreover, if patrol craft attempt to pursue
Japanese fishing vessels outside the area, as they are
authorized to do, they will also run the risk of seizure
by Soviet and Chinese patrols.
The Natural Resources Section on August 1 requested
the opinion of this Mission as to the advisability of
establishing a United States Navy Patrol in the East
China Sea for the purpose of discouraging Chinese
seizures of Japanese vessels. While agreeing with the
need for effective means to prevent such seizures, this
Mission has taken the position that a naval patroi by
the United States would inour the possibility of
complicated incidents and at the same time raise the
question of taking similar action against Soviet and
possibly other seizures As an alternative to the
proposed use of Unite States vessels to patrol the
East China Seu, this Mission has suggested that General
Headquarters reguest the Department to approach the
Chinese Govern ent directly in an effort to secure
proper recognition of 3CAP as the sole authority governing
Japanese ectivities on the high seas, the cessation of
further seizures by chinese patrols, and the release of
all vessels together with their crews now held by the
Chinese authorities Action on this question is still
under consideration in General Headguarters.

It is only too apparent that Soviet and Chinese
authorities are using alleged violations of the fishing
area and consequent seizures of Japanese craft as a
pretext for achieving other objectives. Soviet seizures
of Japanese craft are obviously serving as a device to
attempt to secure General Headquarters recognition of
Soviet territorial sovereignty in the South Sakhalin
and Kuril-Habomai areas (this Mission's despatch no. 416
of June 27, 1949). Chinese seizures are for the obvious
purpose of acquiring serviceable vessels for the use
of the Chinese Nationalist forces. Most of the fishing
vessels of the Shimonoseki fishing fleet which have been
victims of Chinese seizures are of steel construction,
diesel powered, and of 80 to l00 tons. Confiscation of
these craft, as provided by the Chinese Executive Order
of August 5, 1948, is tantamount to piracy.
Reports of Chinese seizures of Japanese fishing
vessels and reported instances of such craft being fired
upon and sunk by Chinese patrols have already appeared
in the Japanese press. The Natural Resources Section
has become particularly concerned over this matter and
was apparently responsible for information reaching the
press concerning this Mission's protest to the Chinese
Mission. A copy of an article on this subject from

CHINESE MISSION IN JAPAN
TOKYO
MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, May 14, 1949.
Reference is made to the Memorandum of the
Diplomatic Section, GHQ, SCAP, dated January 24,
1949 requesting information concerning a law
published in the August 29, 1948 issue of the Chinese
newspaper Ta Kung Pao, Shanghai, which allegedly
contains measures to be taken against Japanese fishing
vessels operating on the high seas outside the SCAP-
authorized fishing areas.
The Chinese Mission has referred the above inquiry
to its home Government for instructions and is now
directed to make the following reply:
1. The so-called "law", entitled "Measures
Dealing With Japanese Fishing Vessels Operating Beyond
Authorized Areas", is, strictly speaking, not a law,
nor is it passed by the Legislative Yuan as alleged.
As a matter of fact, it is in the nature of an executive
order, issued by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
and approved by the Executive Yuan, which became effective
from the date of approval on August 5, 1948. Article 6
stipulates that these Measures are equally applicable to
any Japanese fishing vessel captured prior to that date.
2. According to Articles 1 and 2 the application
of these Measur es is limited to those cases of Japanese
fishing vessels found operating beyond the SCAP-authorized
areas which are to include those areas lying between
China's territorial waters and the high seas in which
China has hitherto had traditional fishing interests.
These Measures, of course, do not apply to all other
cases of violations by Japanese fishing vessels.
3. The enforcement of these Measures is necessitated
by frequent violations on the part of the Japanese fishing
vessels in unauthorized areas off China coast for more
than two years. This regrettable state of affairs has
persisted in spite of repeated protests by the Chinese
Government. With a view to insuring the prevention of
further violations and to safeguarding China's legitimate
interests, the appropriate authorities of the Chinese
Government have no other recourse than to enforce the
above mentioned Measures.
In

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
1oyu s vosyaon .
553, August 13, 1949.
-2-
In this connection attention may be invited to a
revised draft of the various proposed policies on fishing
and acquatic industries in Japan (27l series) as prepared
by the Fishing Subcommittee of the Far Eastern Commission
and approved by the majority of the members of the said
subcommittee. Paragraph 2b of the draft provides the
following condition concerning possible expansion of the
present Japanese fishing activities:
If such expansion will involve fishing or
other commercial acquatic activities in an area
in which any member country of the FEC has informed
SCAP it has a direct concern in respect thereto,
prior authorization shall be obtained by SCAP
from such country。
Since Japanese fishing vessels have been expanding
their activities in the areas off China Coast and outside
SCAP-approved limits without China's prior consent,
such activities are clearly unauthorized and consequently
detrimental to China's legitimate interests.
The above quoted draft also contains the following
provisions:
The Far Eastern Commission recommends to
its member governments that in order to assist
in the enforcement of this policy they should
notify SCAP of violations discovered by s uch
member governments, without prejudice to any
action which they deem fit to take.
The Chinese Government holds that the Measures
preventing violations by Japanese fishing vessels are
entirely in conformity with the letter and spirit of
the above mentioned revised draft of the Far Eastern
Commission.
To the
Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander For the Allied Powers,
Tokyo。.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, presents its
compliments to the Chinese Mission in Japan and has
the honor to refer to the Mission's memorandum of
May 14, 1949, as well as to the Diplomatic Section's
communication of January 24, 1949, concerning the
issuance of an Executive Order by the Chinese Govern-
ment authorizing the seizure of Japanese fishing
vessels operating outside the fishing area established
by the Supreme Commander, and seizures by Chinese
authorities of Japanese fishing vessels reportedly
found out side the fishing area on the high seas.
In its communication of May l4, the Mission
has invited he Diplomatic Section's attention to
a revised draft of a proposed policy decision on
fishing and acquatic industries in Japan, forwarded
to the Steering Committee, Far Eastern Commission,
by Committee No. 2: Economic and Financial Affairs,
at its 144th meeting on March 15, 1949, (SC 271 series)
as evidence to justify the action taken by the Chinese
Government in issuing the Executive Order and seizing
Japanese fishing vessels.
With respect to the policy proposal cited by the
Mission in its memorandum of May 14, it must be noted
that this document is merely a working paper which has
not as yet been adopted by the Far Eastern Commission
as a statement of the Commission's policy on these
matters. Moreover, it should be noted that in a recent
revision of this proposed policy decision, paragraph 6
thereof, quoted in full in the Mission's communication,
has been deleted in its entirety from the draft.
It can not be considered that the Mission has any
valid justification for using this draft policy decision,
still unapproved by the Far Eastern Commission either
in fact or in principle, as evidence to demonstrate
that the Chinese Government's action in issuing the
Executive Order and in seizing Japanese fishing craft
is justified by or in conformity with the policies of
the Far Eastern Commission. It must also be pointed
out that the Executive Order directing the seizure of
Japanese fishing vessels was issued, according to the
Mission's communication, on August 5, 1948, whereas the
Far

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 2 to Tokyo'
Despatch no. 553, August 13,
1949。
-2-
Far Eastern Commission's proposed policy paper was not
circulated for consideration within the subcommittee
until January 10, 1949. This action of the Chinese
Government in issuing the Executive Order of August 5,
l948, could not, therefore, have been taken with reference
to the proposed policy paper, and must be regarded as
a unilateral action on the part of the Chinese Government
for which there is no justification.
It should further be noted that, although the proposed
policy paper was forwarded by the committee concerned
to the Steering Committee of the Far Eastern Commission,
the United States has maintained a general reservation
relative to the entire paper, and the Union of Socialist
Soviet Republics has maintained a general reservation
with particular reference to paragraph 2, b, a part of
which is also quoted in the Mission's communication.
It should be apparent, therefore, that this proposed
policy paper is entirely irrelevant. Unless this paper
is approved by the Far Eastern Commission as a whole and
a pertinent directive is received by the Supreme Commander,
it does not determine the Supreme Commander's position or
policy vis-a-vis the enforcement of his directive
authorizing Japanese fishing and whaling operations on
the high seas.
In the Diplomatic Section's communication to the
Mission of June 22, 1948, it was specifically stated that
"In as much as fishing activities of Japanese on
high seas are governed by directives issued by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and which are
subject to enforcement by the Supreme Commander only,
the competent authorities are desirous that information
be supplied as far as practicable in the form prescribed,
in order that adequate prosecution can afford appropriate
protection to the legitimate interests involved."
In the Diplomatic Section's communication to the Mission
on January 24, 1949, it was again stated that "Japanese
fishing activities on the high seas are governed entirely
by directives issued by the Supreme Commander which are
accordingly subject to enforcement by the Supreme Com-
mander only." In this communication the Mission was also
reminded of the desire of t he Supreme Commander that,
in the e vent of violations by Japanese of the Supreme
Commander's directive governing fishing on the high
seas, the Mission report such cases to the Supreme Com-
mander with information of sufficient probative value
that the offenders can be effectively prosecuted.
The Far Eastern Commission has, so far as is known,
made no policy decision which has resulted in altering
in any manner the above policy.
The

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch no. 553,
August 13, 1949.
-3-
The unilateral action of the Chinese Government
in seizing Japanese fishing vessels on the high seas
has been taken by the Chinese Government without the
consent, sanction, or notification of other Allied
Powers, or without approval of or discussion with the
Supreme Commander to whom the Allied Powers have dele-
gated sole jurisdiction over Japanese affairs. The
Government of China has never been given and can not
justly assume jurisdiction over Japanese on the high
seas, and the area lying between Chinese territorial
waters and the area authorized by the Supreme Commander
for Japanese fishing operations can not be regarded
as subject to Chinese control regardless of "traditional
fishing interests" claimed by China.
On behalf of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, the Diplomatic Section must make emphatic
protest against the unilateral and unjustified action
the Chinese Government has taken in its repeated seizures
of Japanese fishing vessels on the high seas in disregard
of the Supreme Commander's policy which was clearly
expressed to the Chinese Government, through the Mission,
as early as June 22, 1948. It is requested, therefore,
that the Mission convey to the Chinese Government at
the earliest opportunity the extreme ∞ ncern oi the
Supreme Commander over this matter and his desire that
seizures of Japanese vessels cease immediately, and that
all vessels previously seized and still in the custody
of the Chinese Government be released immediately and
returned to Japan together with their crews. The Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers furthermore requests
the Mission to obtain from the Chinese Government a
complete report of all seizures to date, giving full
details surrounding each seizure and the charges against
vessels and crews. As previously noted, if such informa-
tion determines that the vessels have violated the Supreme
Commander's directive by proceeding beyond the limits of
the authorized fishing area, those responsible for such
violations will be brought to trial in Japan by the
competent authorities.
Tokyo, July 18, 1949.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Ailied Powers, presents its
compliments to the Chinese Mission in Japan and has the
honor to bring to the Iission's attention the reported
seizure by Chinese naval craft of four Japanese fishing
boats。
It has been bro ught to the attention of the
Diplomatic Section that in May, 1949, four Japanese
fishing vessels, the Nitto Maru No's. 5, 25, 52 and 53,
were seized on the high s eas by a destroyer of the
Chinese Navy.
Reliable information has been received that the
four Japanese fishing vessels were seized by the destroyer
Chang Chih (
) of the Chinese Navy, that the
vessels with their crews have been held at Tinghai,
Chushan Island, since May 25, 1949, but may be taken to
Formosa, that the vessels are being used for military
purposes, their names having been altered or changed,
and that of the four vessels, the Nitto Maru No. 5 and
the Nitto Maru No. 53 have been lightly armed.
The Supreme Commander views this situation with
extreme concern. As the Mission was advised by the
Diplomatic Section's communication of January 24, 1949,
Japanese fishing operations on the high seas have been
authorized by a directive issued by the Supreme Commander
and in the event that Japanese fishing vessels violate
the Supreme Commander's directive by proceeding beyond
the limits of the authorized fishing area, it is the
responsibility of the Supreme Commander as the sole
executive for the Allied Power, not the responsibility
of any Allied Power individually, to deal with such
violations and take whatever steps are necessary to
insure enforcement of the Supreme Comnander's directive.
It would be appreciated, therefore, if the Mission would
advise the Chinese Government as soon as possible of
this matter and convey the request of the Supreme Com-
mander that the four Japanese vessels which were seized
on the high seas by the Chinese destroyer Chang Chih
and any other Japanese vessels which may have been
similarly seized, be released immediately and returned
to Japan together with their crews.
In

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 3 to Tokyo's
Daspatoh no: 553, August 13,
1949.
-2-
In view of the reported use of some of these
vessels for military purposes, the Supreme Commander
additionally requests that the Mission secure a full
report on this incident. In addition to an explanation
of the reported use of these vessels for military purposes,
it is requested that the report give such particulars
as the date and position of each seizure. If it is
determined that the vessels in question were outside
the authorized fishing area, the persons responsible
for this violation of the Supreme Commander's directive
will be brought to trial in Japan by the competent
authorities.
Tokyo, June 27, 1949.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Ailied Powers, presents its
compliments to the Chinese Mission in Japan and has
the honor to refer to the Diplomatic Section's
communications of June 27, 1949 and July 18, 1949,
concerning seizures of Japanese fishing vessels by
Chinese patrols.
It has been brought to the attention of General
Headquarters that seven vessels additional to those
mentioned in the Diplomatic Section's above-cited
communications have been seized by the Chinese Navy.
The vessels involved consist of Tokai Maru and Miho
Maru, seized on or about May 13; Hokoku Maru Nos. 58
and 60, seized on or about June 11; Koyagi Maru No. 5
and Zenshin Maru Nos. 3 and 5, seized on or about
June 10-11. Miho Maru escaped and returned to Japan
on June 4. Hokoku Maru Nos. 58 and 60 were apparently
taken to Penghau Island on June 12. Crew members of
Koyagi Maru No. 5 and eight crew members of Zenshin
Maru No. 5 were transferred to Zenshin Maru No. 3
which was ordered to return to Japan, arriving on
June 15。
As the Mission was advised in the Diplomatic
Section's communications of June 27 and July 18, the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers views with
grave concern the continued and unwarranted seizure
of Japanese fishing vessels on the high seas by Chinese
naval patrols, and particularly the use of such vessels
by Chinese naval forces.
On behalf of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers. the Diplomatic Section must again make emphatic
protest against this unjustified action and again request
the Mission to inforn the Chinese Government that the
Supreme Commander desires that such seizures of Japanese
fishing vessels on the high seas cease herewith and that
all vessels heretofore seized be released and returned to
Japan with their crews together with a complete report
of the circumstances surrounding each seizure.
Tokyo, August 8, 1949.

Nippon Times, August 1, 1949.
SEBALD PROTESTS NATIONALIST GRAB OF FISHING BOATS
Details Being Withheld by SCAP Official - 1l Vessels
Involved.
By the United Press.
SCAP's fisheries chief, W. C. Herrington, said
Sunday that the Occupation headquarters does not plan
to arm the new Japanese fisheries inspection fleet to
prevent further Nationalist Chinese attacks on Japanese
fishing boats. Foreign commentaries on General MacArthur's
diplomatic protest against the se'zure of ll Japanese
fishing vessels by Chinese gunboats in the East China
Sea have suggested that the Japant se inspection fleet
might be armed and prepared to retaliate if the Canton
government does not insure that the Nationalist forays
will cease.
By John Rich
INS Staff Correspondent
General MacArthur's Diplomatic Section Chief William J.
Sebald confirmed Saturday that he has protested to the
Chinese Nationalist Government against seizure of Japanese
fishing boats in waters southwest of Kyushu.
Mr. Sebald declined to discuss the written protest in
detail "while negotiations are still going on" but admitted
that it involved eleven vessels.
W. C. Herrington, head of SCAP's Fisheries Division,
said returning Japanese fishermen had reported the
captured boats were being put to military uses.
It was believed this point was also included in the
diplomatic protest.
Mr. Herrington said, according to information from
fishermen, boat owners and the Japanese government,
the Chinese are holding nine trawlers and seven full
crews at an undisclosed port or ports.
He said two boats, with two extra crews, had
escaped and returned to Japan.
The Japanese trawlers, operating out of. the ports
of Shimonoseki, Nagasaki and Tobata are steel hulls,
diesel propeller, of about lo0 tons and carrying a
crew of 14.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 5 to Tokyo's
Despatch no. 553, August 13,
1949.
-2-
He said the captured boats had been picked up
over the past two months both inside and outside Japan's
legal fishing areas in a latitude about parallel with
Shanghai.
Mr. Sebald said his protest handed to Chinese
General Chu Shih-Ming did not deal with the reported sinking
of a Japanese fisherman recently southwest of Kyushu by
an unidentified gunboat.
Survivors who reached Japan this week reported
their skipper and four crew members were killed outright
by gunfire from the war vessel which sank the trawler.
The Japanese Maritime-Safety Commission said the
survivors will be brought to Tokyo next week for question-
ing。
SCAP spokesmen have been publioly critical of Japanese
fishermen for consistently poaching outside the restricted
fishing grounds set aside for Japan after the surrender.
This month SCAP directed the Japanese Government
to provide all the boats necessary to establish an
effective patrol to keep Japanese fishermen within their
home grounds.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS

NR 432.11 (ε Au9 49)Fi
8 August 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR: Record
SUBJECT:
Capture and Sinking of the Akashi-maru
No. 105
1. On 2 August 1949 a meeting was held in Fisheries
Division Office to obtain the details of the sinking
of the Akashi-maru No. lo5. Present were:
Mr. T. Ohata, Chief Engineer of Akashi-maru
No. 105
Mr. T. Sei. Taiyo Gyogyo K.K.
Mr. T. Ho ur, Taiyo Gyogyo K..
Mr. J. Ishizeki, Interpreter, Taiyo Gyogyo K.K.
Mr. S. Suzuku, Japanese Fisheries Agency
Mr. T. Kanagawa, Maritime Safety Board
Mr. W. C. Neville, Fisheries Division, NRS
Mr. S. Nagata, Fisheries Division, NRS
Mr. W. C. Herrington, Fisheries Division, NRS
2. In response to guestioning Mr. Ohata reported
as follows:
a. The Akashi-maru No. 105 was a steel bull
trawler of 76 gross tons, pavered with the 160 horsepower
hot bulb engine and with a speed of 8.5 knots. She was
owned by the Taiyo Gyogyo K.K. This boat and her partner,
Akashi-maru No. 103, left Shimonoseki on 1500 hours of
30 May. On the 2nd of June at 1600 hours they reached a
point estimated to be 290 19' N. 1240 19' E with soundings
of 8 m. and began fishing. On the 9th of June while
in the region 270 30' N and 1240 10' E at 1935 hours
they began cruising in search of better fishing grounds
and at 2230 hours Akashi-maru No. 105 1ost sight of No. 103.
Mr. Ohata was in the engine room most of the time so that
much of his story is a repetition of information obtained
from the captain before his death. In searching for
Akashi-maru No. l03 . vessel ran west, probably outside
of the authorized fishing area. At about Oloo hours,
10 June

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
toure no. . to
Tokyo's no. 573,
August 23, 1949.
-2-
10 June 1949, several lights were seen indicating other
vessels were fishing in that area. A light was seen
approaching and the Akashi-maru No. 105 became suspicious
and changed course to the east. The approaching boat
then turned a searchlight on the Akashi-meru No. 105
and fired a shell across her bow. The Akashi-maru No. 105
stopped her propeller and the other ship came along side.
b. The pursuing ship carried no flag and had no
markings which could be seen by Mr. Ohata. She was about
twice the length of the keshi-maru No. 105 and her deck
was about four feet above that of the Akashi-maru No. 105
which has a freeboard of about five feet. On the after-
deck of this ship which was alone side the Akashi-mari
No. l05, Mr. Ohata saw a gun luiret vi th two guns just
aft of the funnel. He did not see the forward part of the
boat so oes not know what armament was carried forward.
On the stern this bost carried  stack of chairs and
tables. The ship was gray, somewhat darker than the color
of U. S. warships.
c. One man boarded the Akashi-maru No. 105 with
the end of a 2/4" wire rope which he ordered the Japanese
crew to secure to the bow. He walked forward with two
Japanese fishermen to make fast the tow cable and remarked
to them in Jaranese, "You have had hard luck.' This man
spoke Jepanese without an accent and wore a uniform and
cap of dark khaki with two dark lines on the cap, which
appeared to Mr. Chata to be similar to that worn by the
prewar Japenese Navy. No other members of the crew of the
attackinc boat came on board the Akashi-maru No. 105,
but Mr. Ohata heard then telking among themselves and all
spoke Chinese. Mr. Oheta has spent several years at
ingtao and can distinguish between the spoken Chinese
and orean. He also stated that the faces of the crew of
the other boat were definitely Chinese. Three of the
Chinese crew wore officers caps, 3 wore caps with turned
up ear flaps and two wore typical sailor's caps. The
Japanese speaking officer then returned to the Chinese
boat and shortly afterward a note was handed down written
in Chinese-Japanese characters. Mr. Ohata glanced at
this note and then handed it to the captain. He saw
that it indicated directions for four speeds - full,
half, stop and one more which he could not make out.
d. The Chinese Boat signaled full speed ahead
and, with the assistance of the tow provided by the
Chinese boat, a speed was reached which Mr. Ohata estimated
to be 16 or 17 knots. At ahout 0300 hours on 10 June 1949,
the towing wire snapped and the Chinese boat swung around
and through a meguphone ordered the k shi-maru No. 105
to follow at full speed. When the latter approached
too close, a shell was fired across her bow; if she
dropped back, a shell was fired across her stern.
e. At

e. At loooo hours continuous shelling began
while Mr. Ohata was in his bunk, which he left and came

the ship began sinking. This was delayed temporarily
by the bulkhead separating the engine room from the
forward and after hold. While the ship was sinking,
the Chinese boat began sweeping the hull with machine
Hhfire.  The No. 1 fireman was hit by shell fragment.
and others were wounded. When machine gunning stopped,
Mr. Ohata came on deck and the men began attempting to
put together a raft.
f. At l0lo hours eight men jumped from the vessel
as the engine room flooded, leaving four men apparently
killed. Three of the escaping men were badly wounded;
one in the thigh, one in the back and one in the head.
Following this the shooting stopped and the Chinese boat
circled back past the debris while the Japanese survivors
hid. A raft was made up of the floating debris but six
hours later broke completely and the fragments were
divided among the eight men. Four of these remained in
one group and two were s eparated somewhat from the remainder,
while the other two disappeared during this time.
g. At about 2300 hours a light was sighted and
the surviving fishermen shouted to attract attention.
Four survivors were picked up at 23lo hours Hong Kong
time by the SS. Christo Par of Panama and brought to
Hong Kong. The two others disappeared before they could
be picked up. At 1400 hours on the 12th of June they
reached Hong Kong and remained on the boat until 12o0
hours on the 13th of June when they landed and were held
in a refugee camp. They left Hong Kong on the l4th
of June on the British steamer San Goro and on the 19th of
June arrived at Kobe.

by the Chinese boat, Mr. Ohata discussed the situation
with Capt. Morita of the Akashi-maru No. l05. The captain
believed that they were still in the authorized area
when sighted by the Chinese boat.

GENERAL FEADGUARTERS
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, presents its
compliments to the Chinese Mission in Japan and has
the honor to refer to the Diplomatic Section's communica-
tions of June 27, July 18, and August 8, 1949, concern-
ing seizures of Japanese fishing vessels on the high
seas by Chinese patrols.
A further serious incident for consideration in
connection with those previously reported has now been
brought to the attention of General Headquarters, namely,
the case of the Akashi Maru No. l05, which is reliably
reported to have been forcibly seized by an armed
Chinese vessel in the Zast China Sea on June 10, 1949,
taken in tow by the Chinese and later sunk by gun fire.
While the Akashi Maru No. 105 was sinking, the Chinese
vessel swept the fishing craft with machine gun fire.
The captain of the fishing vessel and three members of
the crew were killed by machine un fire, four crew
members are missing and are presumed to be dead, and
four crew members survived the attack. The survivors
were later rescued by a British vessel, taken to Hong
Kong, and repatriated to Japan on June 19.
It is requested that the Mission communicate the
foregoing to the Chinese Government for consid eration
in connection with the communications under reference
and that the Di lomatic Section be provided as soon as
may be possible with eny information which may be avail-
able in the premises, as well as the views of the Chinese
Government.
Tokyo, August 23, 1949.

I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatoh
no. 416 of June 27. 1949, concerning Soviet and Japanese
territoriel claims to the Kuril Islands and the Habomai-
Shikotan Group.
The Natural Resources Section, General Headquerters,
has requested this Mission's views concerning a proposel.
now being discussed for a northern extension of the author-
ized Japanese fishing area as established by SCAPIN l033
as amended The present boundaries of the fishing area
1/
and the proposed extension are indicated on the enclosed
sketchThe present boundary in northern Hokkaido would
be extended to the middle of the Soye (La Perouse) Straits
between Hokkeido and Sakhal in, extending eastward to the
point 1490 10' east longitude and 450 44' north latitude;
thence south, passing the midpoint of the Etorofu Straits
between Etorofu Isleni and Uruppu Island in the southern
Kuri1 Islands, to the point l490 l0' east longitude and
450 north latitude, thence east to the northeastern oor-
ner of the present bound ery.
The proposed extension would eliminate the unsatis-
factory boundery now passing through the Nemuro and
Goyomai Straits which provides the Japanese in easten
Hokkeido only a three-mile belt as a fishing area a
as a navigation channel between Hokkaido and the Kuril
and Habomai Islands. The proposed extension would so
make evailable to the Japanese excellent fishing grounds
in the waters between Kunashiri Island and the Habom
Islands which are now outside the authorized fishing
FAP
rea. It is in this area that many of the Soviet sei-
zres of Japanese fishing craft have taken place.
By exnding
SECRET
RETURN TO D

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 594,
August 29, 1949.
-.2 -
By extending the boundery to the proposed limits,
Soviet-occupied Etorofu, Kunashiri, the Hobomai Group,
and Shikotan Islend wouid be brought within the author-
ized fishing erea. Paragraph 3a of SCAPIN 1033 provides
that
"Tepanese vessels will not approach closer than
twelve (12) miles to any island within the author-
ized sree which lies south of 3oº North Latitude.
with the exception of Sofu Gan. Personnel from
such vessels will not land on islends lying south
of 3o0 North Latitude, except Sofu Gan, nor have
contact with any inhabitants thereof."
Paragreph 3b also imposes this twelve mile limit to the
island of Takeshima which is north of 300 north latitude.
Although the twelve mile limit is at present restricted to
specific islands within the authorized rishing aree (prin-
cipally the Bonin and Ryukyu Islends which are under United
States occupetion), this provision of SCAPIN 1033 is be-
lieved to offer an undesirable precedent for the Kuril and
Habomai Islands if these islends were brought within the
fishing erea by the proposed northern extension. The ap-
plicetion of e twelve mile limit to the Kuril-Habomai is-
lands would in effect negate any fishing advantages which
might be gained by the proposed extension, since large
parts of the best fishing arees would be cut off due to
the close proximity of the Soviet-occupied islands. More-
over, if the twelve mile limit were literelly epplied with
respect to Suisho Islenl in the Habomai Group, it would be
impossible for Tapenese vessels to use the Goyomai Straits,
the width of which between Noseppu Cape in Hokkaido end
Suisho Island is only approximately three miles.
In discussing informally the proposed northern exten-
sion with the Naturel Rescurces Section, this Missionghas
expressed the view that action on this matter be postponed
until current discussion of the Japanese fishing area prob-
lem in the Far Eastern Commission more fully clarifies the
policy of the United States and the Commission. It has al-
so been suggested that the present twelve mile limit of
SCAPIN l033 presents a problem which might complicate the
proposed northern extension, and that bafore such an e
tension is taken up it would be edvisable to give considera-
tion to emending CAPIN 1033 so that the twelve mile limit
is modified to the customary three mile limit. Such a pre-
liminary step would only affect territories now occupied
by the United States
While the likelihood of strong Soviet opposition to
the proposed northern extension of the fishing area is
fully recognized, this Mission believes thet it is neces-
sary end desireble to effect some kind of rectification
of the northern boundaries of the fishing area at the
earliest opportunity. The present boundery in eastern
Hokkaido
SECRET

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 594,
August 29, 1949.
Hokkaido creates an impracticable, unworkable situation
The so-called MacArthur Line deprives the Japanese in
this aree of the only good fishing grounds, a situation
which is conducive to premeditated violations of the 
fishing area however strict patrols and enforcement may
be and however much efforts ere mede to educate the Jepa-
nese to abide by the provisions of 3CAPIN 1033. The re-
wards for violating the provisions are great and are
coupled with a deep-rpoted attitude on the part of the
Japanese that they have been unjustly deprived of one of
their best and treditional fishing grounds. The fact,
too, that much of the Kuril fishing erea is on the high
seas (that is. beyond the three mile limit of any Kuril
territories) gives the Jepanese e further argument in
favor of fishing in these waters.
The present fishing boundary in the Hokkaido-Kuril-
Habomai area elso constitutes a serious hazard to naviga-
tion by vessels which endeavor to abide by the provisions
of SCAPIN 1033 and stay within the MacArthur Line.
The most important ergument in favor of the proposed
northern extension is believed to be found in the future
political implications which have grown out of Soviet oc-
cupation of the southern Kuril Islands and the Habomai
Group While the provisions of SCAPIN 1033 are, strictly
speaking, a SCAP administrative matter having no bearing
on the future disposition of territories or determination
of political boundaries, it is now clear that the Soviet
Government is thinking in terms of the present MacArthur
Line, in the channels bordering eastern Hokkaido, as the
future political boundery The longer this situation is
left unehanged, the more ustified will the Soviet Govern-
ment feel itseif in regarding the MacArthur Line as a basis
for a permanent political boundary Even though Soviet op-
position may be of such charecter as to prevent realiza-
tion of the proposed northern extension, it is believed
that such a proposal should be mede at the earliest oppor-
tunity to show that SCAP does not regard the present bound-
ary ss a permanent, immutable line. Even an unsuccessful
effort to extend the line in this area may serve as a use-
ful precedent when the Tapanese peace settlement is made
end it becomes necessary to draw Japan's future interna-
tional boundaries As the situation now stands, all.ac-
tion taken by SCAP with regard to the northern boundaries.
of the fishing area has paid extreme deference to the So-
viet position, not only in the southern Kuril Islands but
also with regard to the unilateral steps taken by the So-
viet Union in occupying the Habomai Group and the island
of Shikotan, neither srea of which can be properly re-
garded as a pert of the Kuril Islands as pledged to the
Soviet Union at the Yalta Conference.
In view

Tokyo's Despatch No. 594,
August 29,1949.
In view of these considerations end the pending
proposal of the Natural Resources Section, this Mission
would eppreciate the Department's views on the proposed
northern extension of the fishing erea.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

SEATTLE 1, WASHINGTON

Hon. Warren G. Magnuson,

Washington, D. C.
Dear Senat or Magnuson:
The fishing industry has informed me that the Army has annoumeed that effee-
tive October lst, it will discontinue buying fish through the Seatale Quar-
termaster Market Center for shipments to troops of occupation in Japm and
the South Pacific.
Apparently this means that badly needed employment in the Pacifie Northmest
will be shelved for the benefit of the building of the Japanese fishing fleet.
On behalf of thousands of our members who will be affected, we vigerougly
protest this step, and ask that you do everything within your power to give
our employees opportunities to work and earn a living, as employment available
to them is almost dovn to zero, if not working in the industry.
I realize this raises any number of problems, but I believe that Ameriesm
workers should be given first opportunity to whatever work may be available.
The import of foreign fish products and lack of foreign markets is alreedy
putting a great strain on the economy of those communities where fishing
predominates. 65% of the Army's Japanese and South Pacific purohases are
made, at the present time, through the Seattle Quartermaster Market Center.
Please advise on anything you can do in this matter.
Yours eincerely,
JFJ1g
J. F. JURICH

Sept ember 2, 1949.
1. The inistry of Fereign Affairs was duly informed by the Director-
General, Maritime Safety Agency, of the sinking of "No. 105 Akashi Maru"
on June 10, 1949, of the detention of steam-trawler "Miho Maru" and "Tokai
Maru" on May 12, 1949, of the  tention of steam-trawler "Kasho Maru No. 5",
"Zenshin Mru No. 5" and "Ne. ?" on June 10, 1949. The report of above
cases are /sic/ enclosed herewi th.
2. Although the report with regerd to the sinking of "Akashi Maru"
doe not pecify the nationality of the vessel in offensive, it is
logical and reasonable to assune fron the circumstances in which the
other ships were seized that the vessel in question is of Chinese origin.
The report reveals that "No. 105 Akashi Maru" was ordered to steam along-
side by a warship of untnown origin on June 10, 1949 while sailing on
Eastern China Sea. The ve sel attempted to escape, but was forced to
stop after receiving a threatening gunfire. From this point on she was
towed by the warship by means of a cable. However, the cable broke off
and wlthough the ve sel vas following the warship at full speed as
ordered, the vas suddenly fired upon end sank eventually. Four members
of the crew were killed und four others are still missing.
3. Accorling to the report reached at the Maritime Sefety Agency,
the point where Akashi  aru met the warship is clearly within the limits
set forth by the S0Ar Menorandum dated June 22, 1946 concerning the area
authorized for Japanese fi hing and whaling. The Ministry of Foreign
Affairs considers it logical to cone lude that it is implied in the Memo-
randum that no attack will be made by the Allied warships upon a Jap-
anese vessel peacefully engaged in fishing within the authorized area.
4. It app ars, however, that the Chinese authorities claim that
this incident occurred within the territorial waters of China. But even
in the territorial waters, it constitutes a flagrant violation of the
established rules of international law, in war or peace, to have re-
course to armed att ack upon a peaceful vessel in the circumstances in
question,
5. It is the

Akashi Maru" together with other similar incidents deserves full
attention on the part of the Japanese Government and that a strong
protest should be lodged with the Chinese Government in regard thereto.
It vill be highly appreciated, therefore, if the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers take necessary steps to-protest to the Chinese Govern-

With the master dead and the mate missing it is difficult to
trace the exact place of disaster, But, it is inferable, from what
the survivors say, that the vessel was captured somewhere around
Long. 124035' E/ Lat. 28040'N and later vas sunken somewhere around
Long. 1240E/Lat. 29010' N.
4. Casualties:
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 638, September 15,
1949.
-2
4. Casualties: 4 alive, 4 dead and 4 missing.
5. Vessel in offensive.
The vessel in question whose name is unknown was a dark-grey,
one funnelled, about 800 ton warship of unknown nationality. It
was equipped with 4 pieces of what seened to be a /sic 8cm. gun
and 5 pieces of machine gun fore and aft. Abroad /sic were seen
about 20 soldiers, several of whom were armed with automatic rifles.
One of them was heard speaking Japanese with no provincial ac :er t.
6. Outline of Accident:
Together with fellow boat No. 103 Akashi-maru the boat left
Shimonoseki for the fishing ground on 30 May. On 2 June at Long
124035'E/Lat. 29019 N the boat operation sic and went on till
it reached Long, 1240 E/Lat. 27048' N on 6 June, 2000 when it turned
north to change the ground.
While heading north, the boat sight of the fellow boat amidst
many fishing lights around there and went further north in search
for her. The next day at Olo0 it discovered a light in the direction
of northwest and approached to find, instead of her, a warship of.
unknown nationality. "Follow us or you shall be fired" ordered a
voice from the ship in clear Japanese. However, as the boat was
within the MacArthur Line and found no reason to accompany her it
attempted to escape. For about one hour it ran at full speed, but
it could not get off her for it came to undergo intimidating fires from
her and was forced to stop. Then she came in contact with the boat
and towed it forcibly with wires. At about O330 the wires broke under
the high speed and it was ordered to accompany her at full speed.
However, around looo when it was following her it was suddenly exposed
to shots of about 40 sliells and several hundreds of bullets for about
10 minutes. The engine stopped and the following 4 crew died. A dock-
member named KIMoTo died instantaneously in front of the steering-gear,
MORITA, the master. died in his room and boatswain MORIMOTO, seriously
injured in the steering room, was blown off overboard by a shell when
he appeared later on the deck, while chief engineer, YOSHIWARA, was
seriously injured and died in the engine room.
The remaining eight men hurriedly built a raft in the boat which
was going to the bottom. At 1500 the raft, however, was broken up and
the eight men on it were obliged to swim, holding on to its scattered
boards. Striving to keep company /with/ each other they swam westward.
At 17OO HASHIMOTO and YOSHIWARA were left behind, from serious wound
and cold. Around 1800 Apprentice IKEDA and Mate IRIE were also left
behind among waves.
Discovering a vessel with side-light approaching from north, the
remaining four cried for help at the top of their voice.
The vessel
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 68, September 15,
1949.
-3
The vessel stopped at once and sent' a rescue boat by which
Chief Engineer OHATA, Ciler NOMIYAMA, Deck-member KUSUMOTO and
Cook MIYAYOSHI were saved. Meanwhile the searchlight of the rescue
vessel caught sight of IKEDA and IRIE who had been adrift when they
suddenly disappeared into the waters.
It is supposed that they were dragged into water by sharks
which were found in groups around there.
For over one hour utmost effort had been made to find out HASHIMOTO
and YOSHIWABA and other twe, but no trace of the four could be ound.
It turned out that the rescue vessel was a Panama ship named
Cristoval on whose board the four survivors ent ered. Hongkong Port on
12 June were admitted into a hospital for refugees on 15 June. After
staying there for 27 days, they got on board a British ship named
Sungora on 13 July. entered Kobe Port on 19 and returned to their
head office at Shimonoseki on 20 July.

II. Outline of Case.
Sinee starting operat ion en 2 May the two vessels, Tokai-maru
acting as principal and Miho -maru as subordinate, had been engaged in
fishing in the authorized fishing area, when on the date and near the
spot mentioned above anIST type vessel belonging to the Chinose Havy
energed and ordered then to halt by an intimidating fire. Then a
Chinese Naval officer, without giving any reason, took command of the
vessels, dr ve them on a northward trip from the spot of seizure for
about thirty hours and laid them at anchor for two days at Reizan
Island.
15 May: Entered Psintac Port and deprived of 2, C00 cases of fishes.
17 May: About fiftuen Chinese including Naval officers. sailors
and their famllles embarked in to live together.
27 May: With the se Chinese soldiers on board sailed for Shusan
Islands, accempanied by Tokai Maru, Chinese junks and other seven or
so vessels.
Night of the same day. Stopped at Reizan Island.
28 May All the Chinese soldiers on board either landed there or
renoved to junks or a three-masted vessel. There were seen twenty-five
or so vessels full of soldiers.
29 May: An under officer on guard informed that they might leave
for Shusan Island or Okinawa about 2 June In the night all the members
of Miho-Maru deliberated on a method of escape.
30 May: At O300 all the members dared to make their escape. There
were sweeping fires from machine guns from behind, but all escaped in
safety.
4 June: Returned to Tobata.

DCIR S:F
STANDARD PORM NO. 64
RESTRICTED
TO
: Dr. Chapman and Mr. Flory - U/FW
DATE:September 17, 1948
Mr. Barnett - OE
FROM : Mr. E. Castleman - U/FW
SUBJECT: Japanese fishing - FEC
9/17/48
Dr. Koo of the Chinese Embassy called late Friday afternoon
and stated that he would like to set up a meeting of the FEC Fisheries
894.628/9-1749
subcommittee during the week of the 20th. Mr. Castleman informed
Dr. Koo that the final United States instructions were not as yet
aveilable and that it would, therefore, appear preferable to await
such instructions prior to holding the next Fisheries Committee
meeting. Dr. Koo said that he was rather anxious to press forward
on this matter, and wondered whether the committee might meet during
the week of the 20th to discuss aspects of the problem other than
the area, which is the basic point upon which instructions are being
obtained. Mr. Castleman said that he would discuss the matter with
other interested officers of the Department and telephone Dr. Koo
on Monday.
Monday, September 20, 1948.
Ir. Castleman telephoned Lir. Barnett of the Department on Monday
and informed him of the conversation held with Dr. Koo on Friday.
It was agreed that since the question of area was the basic point
of difference and since other related questions could not be resolved
until agreement was reached on area, no useful purrose would be served
in holding another subcommittee meeting until the United States
instructions were ready.
Hr. Castleman telephoned Dr. Koo on Monday and suggested that the
subcommittee not meet again until the United States instructions were
available, since definitive positions on area would be necessary before
further constructive discussion could be had on this problem. Dr. Koo
agreed and asked if United States instructi ons would be ready shortly.
hr. Castleman stated that he could not indicate an exact time but that
this Government was pressing forward as rapidly as possible in the
preparation of instructions, and that he would inform Dr. Koo as soon
as instructions were available. Dr. Koo agreed to delay the next
subcommittee meeting until he had been informed that the United States
instructions were ready, unless pressure was brought to bear by other
members of the committee. Mr. Castleman asked Dr. Koo to let him know
of any such pressure, and Dr. Koo agreed to do so.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: September 23, 1949
SUBJECT:
Inter-Agency Meeting re FEC Fishing Paper.
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Hamilton-FE
Lt. Colonel Hendrick-Army
General Snow-L
Mr. Castleman-U/FW
Mr. Barnett-OFD
Mr. Moseley-NA
Mr. Blakeslee-FEC
COPIES TO:
U/FW Files
94.428
1—1493
A meeting was held in the office of Mr. Hamilton (FE)
on September 23, 1949 to discuss United States plans
relative to the fishing paper under consideration at
the Far Eastern Commission.
The first item discussed was whether any modifi-
cation would be sought with regard to current United
States instructions to the United States FEC Delegatior
6
or whether United States should retain in substance
these instructicns. After discussion, it was agreed
2
that the United States would not modify the substarce
le
of the current instruction.
4t
A discussion was then held on whether the United
States might seek to obtain favorable wtes from as
many of the other delegations as possible, although it
is realized that the United States position apparently
will not have majority support. Mr. Blakeslee indicated
that Canada was willing to support the United States
position if the United States was prepared to inform
the Canadians that the United States and SCAP were in
agreement on the position taken in preliminary treaty
negotiations, namely that Japanese vessels would not
1962
m
fish within 15o miles of the coasts of Canada and the
United States. It was suggested that since this treaty
is now being considered, the United States might offer
Canada
C ONF IDENT IAL

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENT IAL
-2-
Canada such assurances. It was agreed that it would
be unwise to offer such assurances at the moment since
historic United States position relative to freedom of
the high seas outside three nautical miles remains
United States position. This position has not been
altered by the Presidential Proclamation. However,
it was suggested that should we offer the Canadians
assurances that the Japanese fishermen would not come
within 150 miles of the Canadian coast based only upon
the draft treaty and the Sanaac Agreement that any
modification of the Sanaac position or failure to obtain
unanimity of opinion with regard to the treaty within the
United States, and any subsequent fishing by Japan within
150 miles of the Canadian coast might be considered by
the Canadians as an American breach of faith. On the
other hand, should we inform the Canadians that we could
give no assurances that the Japanese would not fish within
150 miles of the Canadian coast, this might very well
prejudice treaty negotiations. In view of the discussion,
it was agreed that no comment would be made to the Canadians
at the moment despite the fact that this might cause
Canadian opposition to the United States position at FEC.
Mr. Blakeslee indicated that the Australian delegation
would be willing to support the United States position
with a minor language modification in 2b of the current
United States language, namely substituting "consult
with a view" for "attempt to obtain". It was agreed
that should the Australian delegation propose this amend-
ment the United States could concur.
Mr. Moseley stated that the Chinese delegation had
charged the United States with a reversal of position with
regard to a contraction of the area moving the westerly
line further from the Chinese coast. Mr. Moseley was
provided the following information for reply: that the
United States delegation at the FEC had consistently
opposed the Chinese suggestion of the contraction of the
area and opposed the paper containing the Chinese amend-
ment being sent to the Steering Committee; also that the
indication given by the United States representative on
the Steering Committee that the United States would accept
the paper with certain modifications not including a
modification of the Chinese amendment was an inadvertent
error on the part of this Government.
Mr. Moseley
CONF IDENT IAL

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.93)
CONF IDENT IAL
-3-
Mr. Moseley also stated that the USSR had inquired
as to whether SCAP had expanded the area recently and
if so, on what authority. Mr. Castleman was requested
to prepare a reply to the USSR.

concerning the area authorized for Japanese fishing and

SCAPIN 2046 res01n4s S0API: 1033 f Ju 22 1946,
by which the authorized fishing arer was ol iginally es-
tablished, and SCAPI. 1033/1 of Dece ber -3, 948, by
which the boundery of the fishing aree in the aters
between Hokkaido and the Habomai Islends was e ac
curately defined. By the new memorsndum to the Japenese
Government, the author ized fishing erea is extended east-
ward from 1650 east longitude to 1800 east longitude be-
tween 240 and 400 north latitude. In allition Tapanese
vessels operating within the fishing aree are hew per-
mitted to approach to within three miles of any land area
not under the present &dministration of the Japanese Gov-
ernment lying within the authorized fishing ares. Under
SCAPIN 1o33 Japanese vessels were not permitted to approach
closer than twelve miles to any non-Japanese territory with-
in the fishing area. The only land areas presently affected
by the change from a twelve- to a three-mile limitation are
the Ryukyu and Bonin Islands.
In a statement to the press on September 21, r. W. .
HERRINGTON, Acting Chief of the Natural Resources cction,
said that the extension of the aree and the reduction of
the twelve-mile to a three-mile limitation were authorized
"in recognition of the effective work being done by the
Japanese Government and fishing7 industry to control vio-
lations and over-fishing". The Natural Resources Section
NC
estimetes
CONFIDENTIAL
ACTION COP
RETURN TO DO/R PILES WITHIN 14 DAYS. WITH A NOTATION OF ACTIÓN TAKEN.

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No.665,
dated September 23, 1949.
estimates that the new waters added to the fishing area
will produce between 60 and 70 million pounds of fish an-
nually, principally albacore and tuna. While this in-
crease represents only a small percentage of Japan's an-
nual catch, estimated at seven billion pounds, tuna and
albacore are classified as luxury fish which not only
bring high prices in the domestic market but are also
suited for export.
Mr. Herrington also indicated in his statement to
the press that patrol craft of the newly-established
Fisheries Inspection Fleet will patrol the new area.
He also revealed that 33 Japanese fishing vessels have
been seized by Chinese patrols and that two heve been
sunk, while at least ten Japanese craft have been seized
by Soviet patrols. Mr. Herrington further stated that
some of the Japanese vessels confiscated by the Chinese
were within the limits of the authorized fishing area at
the time of seizure (this Mission's despatches no. 553
of August 13, no. 573 of August 23, and no. 638 or Septem-
ber 15, 1949).
874.628
The reduotion of the twelve-mile limitation to three 1349
miles is based in part upon the neoessity to bring Japa-
conformity with internationally accepted principles and
The twelve-mile limitation was originally imposed in
L
SCAPIN l033 primarilv as a security measure, the need for
DLI
which is considered no longer to exist. Moveover, the
twelve mile limit as prescribed by SCAPIN l033 would have 
constituted a serious obstacle in the event it is decided
to extend the fishing area in the Hokkaido-Kuril-Habomai
area (this Mission's despatoh no. 594 of August 29, 1949).
Japanese reaotion to extension of the authorized
2/
fishing area has been most favorable. Five copies of
editorial comment from the Nippon Times of September 23
are enclosed. This editorial especially notes that Gen-
eral Headquarters had previously in June 1949 rejected
a Japanese request for enlargement of the fishing area.
The present action by General Headquarters is, therefore,
interpreted as recognition of the efforts being made by
the Japanese Government and the fishing industry to pre-
vent violations of the fishing area by Japanese fisher
men and to enforce a sound conservation program which
will prevent over-exploitation of the authorized fish-
ing grounds.

Subject:
United States Naval Patrol in East China Sea
OLI
to Prevent Chinese Seizures Japanese Fishing
Boats.
m
DIION OF
NORTHEAST ASIAN AFFAIRS
The Honorable
006611
Secretary of State,
Washington.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Sir:
894.62
With reference to this Mission's despatch no. 553
of August 13, 1949, concerning seizures of Japanese
fishing boats by Chinese patrols, I have the honor to
invite the Department's attention to a proposal made
by the Natural Resources Section of General Headquarters
628/9-2749
that a United States naval patrol be established in the
East China Sea for the purpose of preventing further
seizures of Japanese vessels.
When this suggestion was first broached by the
Netural Resources Section, this Mission in its capacity
of Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters, pointed
out that the establishment of a regular naval patrol
had certain political implications, including the risk
SECRET FILEPR 6 1950
of possible incidents, and at the same time might raise
t question of establishing similar patrols to prevent
Soviet and possibly other seizures of Japanese fishing
vessels. It was suggested by this Mission that, as an
alternative measure, General Headquarters request the
Department to approach the Chinese Government directly
on this matter in an effort to secure proper recogni-
tion of SCAP as the sole authority governing Japanese
activities on the high seas (this Mission's despatch
no. 553 already cited).
FILED
While this Mission did not concur in the proposed
request for a naval patrol, a SCAP radio to the Depart-
ment of the Army (C-52158), August 27, 1949, approved
by the Chief of Staff, requested that the Department be
approached with respect to taking the matter up directly
with the Chinese Government, and, additionally, the De-
partment of the Army was requested to ask the Joint

Chiefs of Steff to direct the Commander of the 7th Task
