SCAP to prevent such seizures. A Department of the Army
radio to SCAP (W-93752), September 20, stated that a
memorandum was being prepared requesting the Department
to take the matter up directly with the Chinese Govern-
ment but asked SCAP whet action had already been taken
against Chinese offenses, to what extent naval forces
under the Commander Naval Forces Far East are being u-
tilized. and what are the intended operations if assist-
ence is to be rendered by the 7th Task Fleet.
The Natural Resources Section prepared an answer to
the Department of the Army's W-93752 which stated in sub-
stance that diplomatic protests have been made to the
Chinese Mission in Japan but have so far been ineffective,
that naval forces under the Commander Naval Forces Far
East have not been used because present commitments of
naval units, involving the Tsushima patrol and other in-
termittent patrols as well as training, precluded the es-
tablishment of & special patrol to prevent seizures of
Japanese fishing boats, and that it is proposed that the
7th Task Fleet establish a regular naval patrol in the
East China See between 260 north latitude and 32030'
north latitude.
This Mission in its capacity of Diplomatic Section
did not concur in this proposed radio.  In a communica-
tion to the Natural Resources Section, September 10, it
was again pointed out that this Mission believed a regu-
lar naval patrol might have serious political consequences
and possibly complicate the current closure of the China
coast and relations wit the Chinese National Government.
In view of these considerations, it was suggested that
the proposed radio indicate that this Mission did not
concur in the esteblishment of a reguler naval patrol un-
less the proposal wes made subject to approval by the De-
partment.
Both the Naturel Resources Section and the G-3 Sec-
tion of General Headquarters disagreed with this Mission's
position, and the matter was referred to the Chief of
Staff for decision on September 16. A copy of this com-
munication is enclosed. The Chief of Staff approved the
radio as proposed by the Natural Resources Section and it
was despatched to the Department of the Army on September
19 (CX-52581).
While full details are not yet available to this
Mission, SCAP's first radio to the Department of the Army
on the subject (C-52158) apparently brought a decision to
undertake & trial patrol operation in the East China Sea
by the 7th Task Fleet. On September 20 the Commander of
the 7th Task Fleet directed the nomination and prepara-
tion of one destroyer for patrol operations over a ten-
day

day period in the East China Sea between the China coast

directs that any attempted violation of Japanese fishing
vessels by any ship either inside or outside the author-
ized fishing area is to be considered. an act of piracy

1. Attention is invited to proposed radio message
to DA in reply to DA message W 93752, concerning establish-
ment of a Naval patrol in the East China. Sea, and to G-3
and DS comments relative thereto, attached as inclosures.
2. SCAP radio message to DA, C-52158, outlined the
situation that exists relative to seizures of Japanese
fishing vessels- in the East China Sea, and requested DA
to esk State Dept to protest to Chinese Government. Radio
also requested that DA ask JCS to direct Commander, 7th
Task Fleet to furnish whatever assistance he could render
SCAP to prevent seizures.
3. DA radio message to SCAP W 93752, states that DA
is preparing a memorandum to State Dept requesting their
assistance, and requests specific information regarding
measures to prevent additional seizures. Substantially,
information requested is (1) what action has been taken
against Chinese offenders, (2) to what extent Naval forces
under CINCFE are being utilized, and (3) what are intended
operations if assistance is rendered by 7th Task Fleet.
4. Proposed reply to DA queries states thet (1) diplo~
metic protests have been made through Chinese Mission in
Tokyo but have been ineffective, (2) Naval forces under
CINCFE have not been used because present commitments of
Navel units, which involve Tsushima patrol and other inter-
mittent patrols in addition to required training, precludes
establishment of a special Naval patrol to prevent seizures,
and (3) that it is proposed the 7th Task Fleet establish a
regular Naval patrol in the East China Sea between 26oN and
32030N。
5. Comments
SECRET

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
despatch no. 675 dated
September 27, 1949.
5. Comments of G-3 and DS, relative to the proposed
radio message, are substantially as follows:
a. DS reiterates their non-concurrence in the
establishment of a Naval patrol in the East China Sea and
proposes that the following be included in the proposed
message: "USPOLAD does not concur in establishment of
regular US Neval patrol for purpose of preventing illegal
seizures of Japanese fishing vessels unless such proposal
is made subject to approval by Department of State."
b. G-3 does not ag ee with DS proposal because
it is felt that, "SCAP is monally obligeted to insure the
safety of the Japanese on the high seas." G-3 concurs in
R proposal reply subject to the exception that instead
of requesting a regular patrol the message be altered, "to
reflect a desire for sweeps incident to normal routine op-
erations through the East China Sea between 260N and 32030
N." Further, G-3 states that, "It is not within COMNAVFE's.
capabilities to undertake this task, therefore outside as-
sistance must be enlisted."
6. MR believes that inclusion of statement proposed
by DS would materially weaken the SCAP position taken in
the original message requesting assistance, and would de-
lay whatever assistance might be forthcoming. Furthermore,
it is believed that JCS will discuss this matter with State
Department prior to committing 7th Task Fleet to additional
duties related to China.
7. NR does not agree with G-3 that the proposal should
be altered to request only patrols incident to normal rou-
tine operations. NR believes that SCAP should request the
full assistance required for effective action in preventing
seizures. If JCS finds it impossible for 7th Task Fleet to
supply assistance requested, then lesser assistance similar
to that suggested by G-3 could be considered.
8. NR recommends that proposed radio message in reply
to DA message W 93752, be approved as drafted.

SUBJECT:
Plan to Use US Navy to Enforce Japanese Fishing Area
Information has been obtained from a telegram (Tab A)
from the Commander of the Seventh Task Fleet that effective
October 23 an operation plan will be put into effect to
patrol the Japanese fiehing area authorized by SCAP. The
plan provides that any attempt to violate the rights of
Japanese fishing vessels by any ship either in or outside
of the authorized area will be considered an act of piraoy,
and that force may be used if necessary to free the Japanese
fishing vessel and escort the offending vessel back to Japan.
It appears from the telegram that the patrol will only be
in those fishing areas near the China coast and not those
bordering on the Kuriles. It is believed that your attenti
should be called to this matter as the Navy action may get 6
us into some, although probably not serious trouble. The 4
Department wae not consulted in this matter.
The decieion to use the US Navy as a patrol is undoubt dly
the result of a previous request from SCAp to the Departmeno
of the Army for assistance in this connection. During 1948
and 1949 a total of 67 seizures. of Japanese vessels both
inside and outside the authorized area by the Chinese and O
the Soviets has been reported. The worst offenders have
been the Chinese who in many instances have held the vessel
for operations in the civil war, and who in some instances O
have resorted to sinking the vessels and killing the crews.4
The Soviets have in some cases not returned the vessels or 0
crews.

General Headquarters
SUPREME COMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Check Sheet
R. V. Teeter, 26-8491
HGS/WCH/RVT/jin
File No. NR 432.1 (1 0ct 1949)Fi
Subject:
Extension of the Northern
Boundary of Authorized Fishing
rea
Note No.
From: NR
To: DS
Date: 1 Oct 1949
1. The need for providing a greater area for Japanese
fishing north of Hokkaido than that currently authorized,
has long been recognized within GHa. SCAP. Not many
months after the original establishment of this lorthern
boundary in late 1945, SCAP initiated negotiations to
regain some pre-war fishing concessions in waters off
Kamchatka and including some canneries locsted in Kamchatka.
USSR refused SCAP's proposal by  erely stating that the
canneries had been confiscated. Negotiations with Washington
on this proposal continued for e "ear, but were finally
discontinued when dashington deoliued to epproach the
Soviet Government to cbtain it o currnce. Again in
May 1948, NR circulated for the coent of the various
staff sections of GHQ a staff study proposing a small
extention of the authorized area to the north of Hokkaido.
Although the need for such an extension was generally
recognized, t he proposal was abandoned because it was
felt that such action at that tiue would perhaps unfavorably
affect the progress of discussions within the FEC on policy
to govern Japanese fishing, as well as Washington's
consideration of a proposed extension of the fishing area
to the south and east.
2. The need for extending the area to the north
remains acute. This action is essential to the rehabili-
tation of the Hokkaido fisheries. Even after long con-
sideration protracted over almost two years, the FEC has
failed to reach agreement upon the policy under which
Japanese fishing areas may be extended, and Washington
has not yet completed consideration of SCAP's proposal
to extend Japanese fishing south into the Trust Area.
In this connection it should be noted that the expected
production to be realized from a comparatively small
extension to the north is greater than that which it is
estimated will be realized if extension into the Trust
Area is approved. Also, these areas, lying just outside
the
CECRET

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
.-2-
NR 432.1 (1 0ct 1949) Fi
C/N 1
the present boundary three miles off the coast of
cont'd
Hokkaido are traditional fishing grounds of the northern
fisher en, which, because of their proximity to Hokkaido,
can be fished at less cost in fuel and materials. It
may be considered that those FEC nations who are fundanentall
opposed to further extension of Jananese fishing are ccgni-
zant of the fact that SCAP wil esitate to take the
initiative to extend Japanese fishing until this polioy
ter is settled in the FEc. The delaying tactics of
any such FEC nations can forestell indefinitely any action
to adjust equitably Japan's fishing boundary at the expense
of Japan & well as the US, who must pay in dollars for
this loss in food production. It is conceivable that
this delay in FEC could continue until the peace settlement.
3. SCAP has announced that Japan is ready for the
peace treaty. NR believes that this fact alone makes
it imperative that some action be initiated to correct the
northern boundary of the authorized fishing area. Unless
ScAf oes on record as advocating the extension of this
oundary before the eace settlement, the USsR will enjoy
the advantage of the precedent established and continued
by SCAP, which will support Russia's probable contention
that this northern fishing boundary be continued as a
political boundary.
4. In consideration of the above, NR has prepared
a Memorandun for the Chief of Staff on extension of the
northern boundary of the authorized fishing area. A
draft of this emorandum is being transmitted to DS for
comment prior to finalizing (Incl 1). DS comments are
especially desired on the draft letter to the Soviet
Member of the Allied Council for Japan (Tab A to Incl 1),
which invited the comments of the Soviet Government
to the proposed extension.

PROBLEM:
To modify the boundary of the SCAP authorized
fishing area north and northeast of Hokkaido,
in the interest of increased production, safer
navigation and better compliance with area
limits.
FACTS BEARING UPON TIE FROBLEM:
1. Ne cran e or extension of the SCAP authorized
fishing area north and northeast of Hokkaido has been
authorized since its establishment, 27 Sept 45 (Tab B).
2. Increased fisheries production is needed to
provide more animal protein for consumption in Japan
and products for export. Before the war an annual average
of 837,000,000 1bs of fisheries products were landed in
Japan by Japanese fishermen from areas where the USSR
now has direct concern.
3. as originally established and later ∞ntinued
in SCAPINs 1033 and 2046 (Tab C), the boundary of the
authorized fishing area off Hokkaido lies three miles
off Soya Hisaki and at a similar distance off the coast
of Shiretoko reninsula and Nenuro Straits. At Nosappu
isaki it lies only 1 l/2 miles from the Hokkaido coast.
4. The proximity of the area boundary to the north
Hokkaido coast denies local fisherien a pess to a large
part of thei. isi ing grounds upon whioh, in the part,
they were dependent for their livelihood, Fishermen's

5. Shoals, rocks and stronz, variable currents in
the narrow waters defined above, make navigation hazardous,
especially when frequent stor s or fogs occur. The risks
involved imperil fishing operations and seriously hinder
enforce ent of SCArIN 2046 by making violation of the

7. The restriction of fishing ressels to the
authorized area requires vessels to take devious courses,
which consume more fuel cil (supplies of which are
critically scarce) than normal sea routes via mid-channel

8. Japenese fishermen, boats and capital developed
the fisheries north of Hokkaido. These fisheries,
particularly those of northern Hokkaido near Soya Misaki
and Nosappu Misaki, are essential to the economic rehabili-
tation of Hokkaido fisheries. The Habomai Group and the
islands of Etorofu and Kuna jiri have been closely bound
to Hokkaido economically for memy years. Although these
fishing grounds are small in area, they are particularly
rich and believed not fully exploited at the present time.
9. Sinee March 1946, GHa, SCA: has received numerous
petitions fron Japanese groups for the authorization of

10. 3CaP has adopted the policy of discussing
proposed exiensions of the authorized fishing area with
countries directly concerned (Tab F). USSR is the
only country directly concerned with the fishing areas
lying just north of Hokkeido
11. The policy of the Us Goverrunent regarding
fisheries on the high seas is to make possible the
maximum production of food from the seas on a sustained
basis. This policy recognizes the right of all nations
to engage in such high s eas rishing provided that they
agree to ebide by international agreenents which may be
deemed necessary for the proper conservetion of the resources
in the area. The US recognizes the righ's of contiguous
nations to set up conservation zones on he high seas, on
the evidence of need, in order to conserve fisheries.
Where the nationals of other countries are involved, such
nations may participate in the jurisdiction over the
conservation regulations for these fisheries. This right
to set up conservation zones does not inlicn e the right
to extend sovereignty beyond territorial wavers or to
exclude any nation from the fisheries conducted within
such conservation zones (Teb G).

north and northeast of Ho kaido should result in an
inerease in fisheries production ranging from 200, 000,000-
400, 000,000 1bs. This inerease in production wili not
only serve to alleviate the severe economic conditions
of the local fisher.en, but will benefit Japan's entire
economy by supplying more food for her people and
increasing exportable conmodities, for which e ready
market exists

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMI ANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Natural Resources Section
AP0 500
Dear General:
The Supreme Conmender for the Allied Powers is
planning to modify the north and northeastern boundary of
the fishing area which he has authorized for Japanese
fishing operations on the high sess, in order to provide
increased production of acquatic prolucts and to alleviate
the serious economic conditions of the north coast fishermen.
The north and norsheastem boundary of the area around Soya
Misaki, the Shiretoko Peninsula snd the Nemuro Straits,
being limited to Japanese territorial waters, bars Japanese
fishermen from nearby high seas fisheries upon which the
prosperity of the local villages depend. Such close
confinement to the Hokkaido coust also subjects Japanese
boats to severe navigational hazards, perticularly in times
of storm and fog. The elimination of these hazards will
allow for etter compliance with the aree limits.
It is planned to modify the boundary of the fishing
area by amending the memorandum for the Japanese Government,
SCAPIN 2046 of September 19, 1949, to inelude the following
coordinates: From the point of 1350E, 400N, north to
140°E, 45°44°N; thence east to 149010'E, 45044 N; thence
south to 149010'E, 450N; thence east to 1650E, 450N;
thereafter following the boundary line as currently established
in above referenced SCAPIN directive.
This proposal is being brought to the attention of
the Soviet Member of the Allied Council for Japen for such
comment as his Government may wish to make.

Natural Resources Section of GHQ has requested POLAD's views

and the Haboma1 Islands. The proposed extension, shown as an
enclosure to Despatch 594 (also attached) should result in an
increase in fisheries production ranging from 200 to 400 million
pounds annually. It would also eliminate the unsatisfactory
boundary with the USsR now passing through the narrow Nemuro and
Goyomai Straits, which restricts Japanese fishing operations in
F
northeastern Hokkaido to a three-mile belt. Since this congested, M
fog-bound belt must also serve as a navigational chennel for
Japanese vessels, navigation end fishing operations in this area
894.628/11

area would also facilitate Japaneee ohservance of the northern
fishing area limits.

the Chief of Staff thet a letter (enclosed with the despatch) be
sent to the USSR representative in Tokyo informing him that SCAP
proposes to extend the authorized fishing area according to the
attached mep together with the principal reasons for this pro-

I woulu like to add the following paragraphs to the

of policy desirability for extending the fishing areas:
Not understood here are statements of
Natural Resources Section (para 2 of Enclosure,
Despatch No. 759) that "SCAP will hesitate to
take the initiative to extend Japanese fishing
until this policy matter is settled in the FEC",
that "the delaying tactics of any such FEC nations
can forestall inderinitely any action to adjust
equitably Japan's fishing boundary at the expense
of Japan as well as the Us," and "that this delay
in FEd could continue until the peace settlement".
Department procceded in FEC negotiations with
understanding that a policy defining fishing areas
was not considered neccbsary by SCAP and that SCAP
preferred to use his executive authority in those
matters. Department was so advised by Dept. of the
Army and in telegram C51375 of July 13 SCAP stated
that "no FEC policy on this subject is neceseary".
For similar reasons no consideration was given here
to question of issuing unilateral directive when
proposed policy decision in FEc was lost because
of Soviet and Chinese vetos.

GENERAL HEAD UARTERS

AIDE MEMCIRE
On November 1. 1949, His Excellency Lieutenant
General CHU hih-ming, Chief of the Chinese Nission
in Japan, called on Mr. William J. Sebald, Chief of
the Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters, to
discuss at the latter's request the subject of seizures
of Japanese fishing vessels by Chinese patrols. Lr. Sebald
referred to the Diplomatic Section's conmunication to the
Mission of October 12, 1949, and previous comrunications,
notably Diplomatic Section's protest against the use of
seized Japanese vescels for military purposes by the
Chinese authorities cont ined in a note to the Mission
dated June 27, 1949, and the Mission's communication
dated August 36. 1949, informing the Diplomatic Section
of the Chinese Navy Head uarters' categorical denial
that seized Jaranese vessels were either being armed or
used for ilitary purposes by the Chinese authorities.
Mr. Seball inforned General Chu that, notvithstanding
the denials just entioned, considerable evidence in
support of the grotest of June 27 has been brought to
the Diplomatie Section's attention. This evidence is of
such probative character as to leave no doubt in the
opinion or General Headquarters that a number of Japanese
fishing vessels seized by Chinese ratrols have been armed
and ure being used by the chinese authorities for military
purposes s one exumple of the evidence now available
to General Meadquarters, General Chu was given a copy of
an affidavit made under oath by a Japanese fishe rman
whose vessel, the stashi Maru No 99, was seized by tvo
armed Chinese patro1 vessels c, August 18, 1949 after
being subjected to machine-sur fire.. Aboard these patrol
vessels, moreover were a number of armed Chinese soldiers.
The sworn affidavit furt er shows that the two Chinese
armed patrol cruft were easily identified by the affiant
as the for er Nitto Maru No 52 und the Tokai Maru,
two Japanose fishing vessels previously seized by
Chinese patrols  The affient was teken to Keelung,
Forosu, after the Akashi Maru No. 99 was captured, where
with other crew members he was eneaged by the Chinese
authorities for repair work on other seized vessels.
The affiant further testified thit while enguged in this
worlk in Keeluns he not only saw a large number.of seized
Japunese fishing vessels, but was able to observe among
them the Nitto Maru Nos. 5, 25, 52, and 53, the Akashi Maru
Nos. 107, 131, 122 und 152, the Tokai Maru, two Taihe1
Meru vessels and two Kaiyo Maru vessels which had been
ar ed by the Chinese authorities.
General

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure no. 3 to
despatch no. 783,
November 12, 1949.
-2-
General Chu was infor ed thct, in v'ew of the
accumulated evidence, General Headquarters must view
with the gravest concern the unwarranted seizures of
Japanese fishing vessels, their unjustified confiscation
by the Chinese authorities, the arming of some of these
vessels, and their employment for military. purposes,
including armed seizure of other Japanese fishing craft.
He was also reminded that the Mission had been infomned
in a number of communications from the Diplomatic Section
that the Supreme Cormander slone, as the sole executive
for the Allied Powers, has jurisdiction over Japanese
vessels on the high seas, and that only the Headquarters
of the Supreme Conmander is entitled to deal with viola-
tion of its directives to the Japanese Governient concem-
ing such matters.
Lotwithstanding the uct that the Chinese Government,
through the ission, was informed of this position as
early as June 22, 1948, t.o Chinese Government on
August 5, 1948, issued an Executive Order providing,
inter alia for the seizure 1 confiscation by the
Chinese Government of any Japanese fishing vessels found
on the high seas between the western limits of the
authorized fishing area and China's territorial waters.
This purported Executive Order not only contravenes the
rules of international law limiting the action of a
sovernment against ships not of its own flag on the high
seas, but also contravenes the recognized responsibilities
of the Supreme Conmander as the sole executive for the
Allied Powers
Ir. Sebald stated that the sontinued disregard
and violetion of its authority through such unileteral
action by the Chinese authorities had becone a matter
of serlous coneern to General Headquarters, end he
accordingly desired to impress upon Generai Chu the need
for urgent and energetic measures by the Mission and by
the Chinese Governm ent to ensure an im ediate cessation
of these lllegal activities. Since repeated protests
against unwarranted sinkings, seizures confisca ions,
ar ing of seized Japanese vessels, ind their use for
military purposes have so far been unavailing, it was
Imperative, he said that core positive means be employed
to urge upon the Chinese Governient the necessity of
its preventing further seizures of Japanese vessels,
releasing the vessels illegally confisocted or detained
together with ell crew members. and siving adequate
assurances that it is prepered to make suitable indemnifi-
cation for the fishing vessels sunk end the lives lost
as e result of ar ed attacks on the high seas by Chinese
patrols. Mr Sebald accordingly requested General Chu
to be so good as to take this matter up with his Govern-
ment at once and to inform him as soon as may be possible
of the action being taken by the Chinese authorities in
the premises
Tokyo, November 1, 1949

DATE: December 12, 1949
SUBJECT:
(1) Extension of Japanese fishing areas
(2) Proposed port privileges convention with Canada
PARTICIPANTS:
U/FW - Dr. Chapman

COPIES TO:

ere
1—1498
I called Dr. Chapman regarding two matters: (1) the pro-
posed extension of Japanese fishing areas and (2) a proposed
port privileges convention with Canada.
(1) Extension of Japanese Fishing Areas - I indicated to
Dr. Chapman that I had discussed this problem with Mr. Leddy,
and that though Mr. Leddy was agreeable to the conditioning of
2
the extension of the Japanese fishing area on the prior working
4
out of adequate inspection arrangements, he questioned the valid-
6
ity of also conditioning this extension on the resolution of the
boundary line problem with the Philippines. I stated that ITP
and CP were concerned that the U.S. position regarding freedom
of the high seas not be compromised, and we have no objection
to this point being made clear in connection with the proposed
extension. I indicated, however, that unless there were strong
political objections of which we had not yet been appraised, we
saw no good reason for going any further and refusing SCAP the
right to undertake the proposed extension until the boundary
problem had been worked out with the Philippines.
68
I inquired

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
I inquired from Dr. Chapman whether this position of
ITP and CP would present any problems to him. He replied
that he thought it would not, since consideration of the
proposed extension was moving forward rather slowly and
he, on the other hand, was working as fast as possible to
develop conservation arrangements with the Canadians. Dr.
Chapman indicated that no further interdepartmental meeting
had occurred since the last meeting held in the Department on
this matter. The scheduled interdepartmental meeting had been
postponed a number of times at the request of Navy. Dr. Chap-
man also indicated that no general departmental position re-
garding the proposed expansion of Japanese fishing areas had
as yet been firmly developed, particularly in view of the
fact that UND is still considering the effect of the expansion
on the Trust territories and the possibility of working out
some arrangements to ensure that the inhabitants of these
territories would gain some benefits in the event any such
expansion of Japanese fishing areas should occur.
(2) Proposed Port Privileges Convention with Canada -
In response to Dr. Chapman's request for comments by December
12 on this convention, I indicated that I did not think we
were going to satisfy the Canadians by granting them port priv-
ileges in Seattle and Alaskan ports but not in any U.S. Pacific
ports south of Seattle, while we were requesting from the Can-
adians port privileges in all their Pacific ports. Dr. Chapman
indicated that the Canadians had a good point in requesting
port privileges along our Pacific Coast generally in return
for a comparable extension of privileges on their part; that
as a practical matter the Canadians would not,however, be
likely to use to any significant degree ports south of Seattle
in any event; that domestic fishing interests did not appear
concerned about allowing the Canadians to use these ports; and
that the principal objection to granting the Canadians priv-
ileges in these ports came from the Customs Bureau in Treasury.
Dr. Chapman stated that he had been trying for some time to
get a clear statement of Customs' objections but as yet he
had not been successful. So far as he could gather, the
Customs people seemed to be concerned principally about two
points: (1) the possible adverse effect competitively on
the domestic fishing industry of giving the Canadians the
port privileges in question, and (2) the problems that might
be raised with respect to granting other foreign countries
similar favorable treatment as a result of the precedent that
might be
CONFDENTIAL

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
might be established should such treatment be accorded Canada.
I indicated that though we would want to look into the
matter more closely, I doubted that we could go along with
the first objection of Customs for not extending port privi-
leges to the Canadians south of Seattle. As for the second
objection, I indicated that I was not sure of all the compli-
cations that might be involved and hence was not in a position
to advance any opinion. I suggested to Dr. Chapman that efforts
be continued to obtain a complete statement of Customs' ob-
jections and that after their views had been obtained, we should
look at the matter again. At such time I would take the matter
up with ITP. Meanwhile, I indicated that ITP and CP were re-
serving their position on the question of extension of port
privileges to the Canadians south of Seattle.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
October 18, 1949
U/FW: Dr. Chapman
U/FW: Isla V. Davies
Discussion with a special advisory group of West Coast
fishery representatives regarding fishery provision in
Treaty of Peace with Japan.
I talked yesterday (October 17) with Mr. Fearey NA to
get his reaction to Dr. Chapman's proposed consultation with
a selected group of fishery industry advisers regarding a
fishery provision in the draft Treaty of Peace with Japan.
I explained to Mr. Fearey that Dr. Chapman felt it necessary,
not to discuss the subject generally with these people, but
to shcw them the exact language of the Article as now
drafted. I said that Dr. Chapman's experience has shown
these people to be trustworthy and if they are satisfied with
the proposed provisions, the remainder of the industry will
be satisfied. I said that Dr. Chapman would not discuss
the matter of timing or programming of any negotiations;
and that this need not occasion any embarrassment since the
West Coast people are themselves raising the question, or
reactivating it, at this time.
Mr. Fearey said it would be entirely agreeable to make
the disclosure of the exact language of the Article, under
the conditions set forth above. He reiterated that the
question of timing must under no circumstances be revealed.
I later (October 18) gave the same information to
SY Mr. Dulin who said he was satisfied to accept Dr. Chapman's
judgment on the point.
